                                                      A groupoidal approach to quantum reference frames
                                                                          Samuel Fedida∗1 , Alberto Ibort†2 , and Arnau Mas-Dorca‡2
                                            1
                                             Centre for Quantum Information and Foundations, DAMTP, Centre for Mathematical Sciences,
                                                         University of Cambridge, Wilberforce Road, Cambridge CB3 0WA, UK
                                             2
                                               Department of Mathematics, Univ. Carlos III de Madrid, Avda. de la Universidad 30, 28911,
                                            Leganés, Madrid; ICMAT, Calle Nicolas Cabrera, 13-15. Campus de Cantoblanco, 28049 Madrid,
                                                                                         Spain

                                                                                                   August 17, 2026
arXiv:2608.14133v1 [quant-ph] 14 Aug 2026




                                                                                                         Abstract
                                                       We develop the kinematical and operator-algebraic foundations of a groupoid-based relational quantum field
                                                   theory on curved spacetimes. The starting point is the observation that the usual group-based quantum reference
                                                   frame formalism is not directly suited to generic curved Lorentzian backgrounds as global symmetry groups are
                                                   typically absent or too small. We formulate a notion of quantum reference frame for a continuous groupoid. This
                                                   yields a groupoid relativization map and relational observables. We show that a localization limit recovers the
                                                   ordinary non-relational description.
                                                       We construct canonical sharp groupoid quantum reference frames, which form the groupoidal counterpart of
                                                   the ideal quantum reference frames based on L2 (G) in the group-based theory. We further prove that the groupoid
                                                   quantum reference frame construction reduces to the standard operational quantum reference frame formalism for
                                                   locally compact groups. The action groupoid quantum reference frames are torsor quantum reference frames only
                                                   for specific classes of fields of positive operator-valued measures, and the torsor relativization map only applies to
                                                   constant operator fields of system observables.
                                                       We review the foundations of relational quantum field theory in Minkowski spacetime. We prove new results
                                                   that further link relational quantum field theory to Wightman quantum field theory. We show that covariant
                                                   positive operator-valued measures are µ-continuous with respect to quasi-invariant σ-finite positive Borel measures
                                                   µ, hence proving that relational quantum fields can indeed be understood as the smearing of pointwise-defined
                                                   kernels with respect to the quantum reference frame’s statistics.
                                                       We develop relational quantum field theory in curved spacetime, where we argue that the correct replacement
                                                   for the Poincaré group is the Poincaré groupoid of the spacetime, i.e. the gauge groupoid of its orthonormal
                                                   frame bundle. We show that the groupoid framework suitably extends the formalism when curved spacetimes
                                                   are considered. We also indicate how the framework extends further to internal gauge symmetry and relational
                                                   gauge-covariant quantum field theory via Atiyah groupoids, providing a first step towards formulating a relational
                                                   quantum Yang-Mills field theory. These results suggest that groupoids provide the natural kinematical language
                                                   for operational quantum reference frames and relational quantum field theory beyond the homogeneous space
                                                   setting.

                                                          2
1     Introduction
In recent years, quantum reference frames (QRFs) have emerged as a mathematically precise way of implementing the
basic relational insight that the description of a physical system should be given relative to another physical system
[1–3], itself treated quantum mechanically [4–19]. In the operational approach [7, 20, 21], a quantum reference frame
is described by a unitary representation of a locally compact group together with a covariant positive operator-valued
measure (POVM) on a homogeneous space of that group. This framework has proved flexible enough to describe
relative observables, relative states, and transformations between quantum frames [19, 21], and it provides a natural
arena in which to formulate symmetry principles operationally.
    In the relativistic setting, Minkowski spacetime fits naturally into this picture. The proper orthochronous Poincaré
group acts transitively on the relevant space of inertial frames, and the operational formalism of QRFs may be
formulated in terms of systems of covariance for that group. This line of thought has led to a relational approach
to quantum field theory in Minkowski spacetime [22], in which relational observables and quantum fields arise as
derived notions from the operational QRF formalism. In that flat spacetime setting, the relevant space of classical
inertial reference frames may be identified with the trivial Lorentz bundle over Minkowski spacetime, and relational
observables are obtained by averaging absolute fields against the localization statistics of a relativistic quantum frame
in that frame bundle. The idea of “quantising" the frame bundle has also been approached by Vanzella and Butterfield
[23], who assigned complex amplitudes (“wavefunctions") to tetrads.
    The situation changes drastically on a generic curved spacetime (M, g). In general, the isometry group of (M, g)
is too small, and typically trivial, to support a useful homogeneous-space description of frames. Thus the standard
group-based QRF formalism no longer applies.1 Nevertheless, curved Lorentzian geometry still possesses a canonical
kinematical object: the bundle
                                                      ↑
                                                    O+  (M, g) → M                                                     (1)
of oriented orthochronous orthonormal frames, and therefore its canonical gauge groupoid
                                             ↑                      ↑
                                            O+ (M, g) ×SO↑ (1,d−1) O+ (M, g) ⇒ M.                                                     (2)
                                                              +


Equivalently, this groupoid may be described intrinsically as
                                                                             ∗
                                                                                                                                      (3)
                                  
                     Poin(M, g) = (y, Tyx , x) x, y ∈ M, Tyx : Tx M → Ty M, Tyx gy = gx ,

whose arrows are linear isometries between tangent spaces at different spacetime points. Following [26], we refer to
this Lie groupoid as the Poincaré groupoid of the spacetime (M, g).
    The central thesis of this paper is that the Poincaré groupoid is the correct replacement for the Poincaré group in
the formulation of operational quantum reference frames on curved spacetimes. More generally, we argue that when
global symmetry is no longer available, one should not abandon covariance, but rather pass from groups to groupoids.
This is a natural extension of relational quantum field theory on Minkowski spacetime for at least three reasons. First,
the Poincaré groupoid exists on every Lorentzian spacetime, regardless of the existence of nontrivial global isometries.
Second, in the flat case it reduces to the familiar Poincaré-group picture. Third, groupoids come equipped with a
well-developed measure-theoretic and operator-algebraic formalism, making them suitable kinematical structures for
quantum theory.
    Our aim is therefore twofold. On the one hand, we extend the operational QRF formalism from transitive locally
compact group actions to continuous groupoids. On the other, we apply that extension to the Poincaré groupoid
of a Lorentzian spacetime, thereby obtaining a groupoid-based framework for relational quantum field theory beyond
the homogeneous space setting. The resulting framework is broad enough to accommodate the notion of operational
quantum reference frames on curved spacetimes; a relativization and relative observables without global symmetry
groups; relational observables and fields on curved Lorentzian backgrounds and, on top of that, further extensions to
internal gauge symmetry via Atiyah groupoids.
   1 We mention here an ongoing project by Głowacki who is exploring a group-based extension of the QRF formalism to principal bundles

[24, 25]. The relativization map in that group-based approach to quantum reference frames applies to operator-valued functions on the
bundle rather than to operators. Understanding the links between the approach of the present paper that deals with groupoids and
observables (and recovers a notion of quantum field as a necessary emergent feature of the relativistic structure of the theory), and that
explored by Głowacki that uses groups and operator-valued functions (where one is relativising already-existing quantum fields rather than
observables), is kept for future work.




                                                                    3
    From a broader perspective, this paper should also be viewed as part of a larger groupoid-based programme in the
foundations of quantum theory [27–29]. The Poincaré and Wigner groupoids have recently been proposed as natural
kinematical structures for the description of particles on curved spacetimes [26]. Here we show that they also provide
a natural geometric setting for quantum reference frames and relational observables. This suggests that particles,
frames, and fields may all admit a common treatment in a unified groupoid language.
    This extension of QRFs from locally compact groups to groupoids should not be seen merely as a mathematical
exercise. We argue that groupoids are fundamentally the correct object to consider in the context of quantum
reference frames when one is concerned with physical symmetries. Some arguments, both conceptual and technical,
to support this claim are as follows.
  1. Only local Poincaré symmetry is well-tested. However, the Poincaré group, and more generally the isometry
     group of a spacetime, restricted to a region U ⊂ M (Minkowski for the Poincaré group) is not a group.
     Thus, we have, strictly speaking, never experimentally tested Poincaré (nor isometry) group symmetry in a lab.
     However, the Poincaré groupoid is still a groupoid when restricted to such regions (and indeed, also works on
     curved spacetimes).
  2. The notion of symmetry is not always global: local symmetries, captured by groupoids, are just as important
     (if not more) than global symmetries, captured by groups but also by groupoids. Working with groupoids thus
     allows one to discuss both global and local symmetries.
  3. When one is interested in the symmetries of a curved spacetime, the isometry group of a generic spacetime
     is trivial. If, however, one wants to talk about the diffeomorphisms of that spacetime, then this becomes
     technically arduous: the diffeomorphism group of a spacetime is not locally compact and typically a very
     complicated object which cannot be straightforwardly integrated into the operational QRF formalism.

  4. To talk about the mass and spin of a quantum field in flat spacetime we typically use the Wigner classification of
     (projective) unitary representations of the Poincaré group with respect to which the quantum field is covariant.
     It was argued in [26] that the same can and indeed should be done in curved spacetimes.
  5. The idea, in relational quantum physics, that “true" observables of a system and a frame are those which are
     invariant under symmetry transformations has been argued from the point of view of the Wigner-Araki-Yanase
     (WAY) theorem [30–33]. This result is often understood in the context of group symmetries (e.g. see [34] for
     a review of those in the context of QRFs), but really is more general than that. It involves the commutation
     of observables with unitaries, which can be taken to be unitary representations of groups. However, one can
     also consider invariance and unitary representations of more general symmetry structures, and in particular of
     groupoid symmetries.

  6. To discuss gauge field theories, one may be tempted to work with gauge groups of the form G = C ∞ (M, G).
     However, these are typically very difficult objects to work with: gauge groups are not locally compact and so
     cannot generically be treated in the standard QRF formalism (see [35] for a recent attempt at studying gauge
     group symmetries in the context of QRFs).
  7. More generally, gauge theories are not fully described by G = C ∞ (M, G), except in a chosen trivialization of
     a trivial principal bundle. In general, the gauge data consist of a principal G-bundle P → M, its associated
     bundles, and its gauge group Gau(P ) containing vertical bundle automorphisms. Nontrivial bundles encode
     topological sectors and holonomy data that are invisible in a global C ∞ (M, G) description. The Atiyah groupoid
     (P ×P )/G ⇒ M is therefore the natural object for working with gauge theory and local gauge transformations.



1.1    Organisation of the paper
The paper is organized as follows. In Sec. 2, we review the geometric and representation-theoretic background
of measure groupoids, with special emphasis on action groupoids, Atiyah groupoids, and the Poincaré and Wigner
groupoids associated with a Lorentzian spacetime.
    In Sec. 3, we recall the operational theory of quantum reference frames for locally compact groups and torsors in
the form most convenient for comparison with the groupoid setting.


                                                          4
    Sec. 4 contains the main conceptual development of the paper: the definition of groupoid quantum reference
frames, the construction of the corresponding relativization maps, and the proof that the formalism reduces to the
usual one in the action-groupoid case.
    In Sec. 5, we apply the formalism to relational quantum field theory, first in Minkowski spacetime.
    We then pass to generic curved spacetimes in Sec. 6, where the relational field is defined intrinsically on the
Poincaré groupoid without any global trivialization of the frame bundle. We show that, in the case where the
spacetime is flat, both pictures concur.
    Finally, in Sec. 7, we indicate how the same logic extends further to internal gauge symmetry via Atiyah groupoids,
leading toward a groupoid-based formulation of relational gauge-covariant quantum field theory.
    Sec. 8 concludes with a discussion of the main conceptual consequences of the paper and several open questions.

1.2    Notation and conventions
All Hilbert spaces under consideration are complex and separable. If H is a Hilbert space, we denote by B(H) the
von Neumann algebra of bounded operators on H, by B(H)sa ⊂ B(H) the self-adjoint bounded operators, by T (H)
the trace-class operators, by D(H) the density operators, and by E(H) ⊂ B(H)sa the convex set of effects. For a
measurable space X, Bor(X) denotes its Borel σ-algebra.
    Given a groupoid Γ ⇒ M , the source and target maps are denoted by

                                                    s, t : Γ → M.                                                  (4)

The identity arrow at x ∈ M is denoted by 1x , and the inverse of α ∈ Γ by α−1 . We write Γ(2) ⊆ Γ × Γ for the set
of composable pairs. If α : x → y, the left translation map is written

                                           Lα : Γx → Γy ,       β 7→ α ◦ β.                                        (5)

    For a d-dimensional Lorentzian spacetime (M, g), the bundle of oriented orthochronous orthonormal frames is
              ↑
denoted by O+   (M, g) → M, and its Poincaré groupoid by Poin(M, g) ⇒ M. In the flat case, Minkowski spacetime
is denoted by M and equipped with metric η of mostly-minus signature. The spacetime translation group is denoted
                                                              ↑
by T (1, d − 1), the proper orthochronous Lorentz group by SO+  (1, d − 1), and the proper orthochronous Poincaré
group by
                                         ↑                    ↑
                                        P+ = T (1, d − 1) ⋊ SO+ (1, d − 1).                                   (6)
    Whenever direct integrals are used, measurability is understood in the standard sense of measurable fields of
Hilbert spaces. All operator-valued integrals are understood ultraweakly in the relevant operator algebras.


2     Continuous groupoids and kinematical representations
In this section we collect the geometric and representation-theoretic background needed in the rest of the paper. Our
main kinematical object is a continuous groupoid Γ ⇒ M , to be interpreted as encoding admissible changes of local
frame on a spacetime or, more generally, local symmetry data. Note that, for most examples of physical interest,
one is interested in Lie groupoids, which are a special (smooth) type of continuous groupoids. The fundamental
example for us is the Poincaré groupoid of a Lorentzian spacetime, together with its phase-space counterpart, the
Wigner groupoid. We also recall the notion of a measurable field of Hilbert spaces and of a unitary representation
of a continuous groupoid, since these will replace the familiar single-Hilbert-space representation theory of symmetry
groups in the groupoid-based formulation of quantum reference frames.

2.1    Lie groupoids, action groupoids and gauge groupoids
We begin by recalling some standard terminology; see for instance [29, 36, 37].
Definition 2.1. A continuous groupoid Γ ⇒ M consists of a topological space of arrows Γ, a topological space of
objects M , continuous maps
                                              s, t : Γ → M                                                  (7)



                                                            5
                                      z1                                  α1            y1
                                                 β1        γ ∈ Γxx
                           Γx = t−1 (x)                                                  Γx = s−1 (x)
                       arrows with target x                   x                      arrows with source x
                                                 β2
                                                                          α2
                                      z2                                                y2

 Figure 1: The source fiber Γx , the target fiber Γx , and their intersection Γxx = Γx ∩ Γx , the isotropy group at x.


called the source and target maps, a continuous unit map

                                                 u : M → Γ,           x 7→ 1x ,                                     (8)

a continuous inversion map
                                               ι : Γ → Γ,           α 7→ α−1 ,                                      (9)
and a continuous partially defined multiplication

                                           m : Γ(2) → Γ,           (α, β) 7→ α ◦ β,                                (10)

where
                                       Γ(2) := {(α, β) ∈ Γ × Γ | s(α) = t(β)},                                     (11)
satisfying the usual associativity, identity and inverse axioms for groupoids.
   If α ∈ Γ satisfies s(α) = x and t(α) = y, we write α : x → y. For x ∈ M we denote

                                Γx := s−1 (x),        Γx := t−1 (x),           Γxx := Γx ∩ Γx .                    (12)

Γxx , also denoted Γ(x), is the isotropy group at x. We write

                       Γyx := Γx ∩ Γy = s−1 (x) ∩ t−1 (y) = {α ∈ Γ | s(α) = x, t(α) = y} ⊂ Γ.                      (13)

   The orbit of x is
                                     Ox := t(Γx ) = {y ∈ M | ∃ α : x → y} ⊂ M.                                     (14)
The groupoid is said to be transitive if it has a single orbit. The isotropy groups Γ(x) and Γ(y) of points x, y lying
in the same orbit are isomorphic; for a transitive groupoid, we write H for the common isomorphism class and refer
to it as the isotropy group of Γ. These notions are represented pictorially in Figs. 1 and 2.
    An important example of continuous groupoids is that of Lie groupoids.
Definition 2.2. A Lie groupoid is a continuous groupoid in which Γ and M are smooth manifolds, s and t are smooth
surjective submersions, and all structure maps are smooth.
Example 2.3 (Action groupoid). Let a Lie group G act smoothly on a manifold M . The associated action groupoid

                                                       G⋉M ⇒M                                                      (15)

has arrows G × M , source and target maps

                                            s(g, x) = x,           t(g, x) = g · x,                                (16)

unit map x 7→ (e, x), inverse
                                                 (g, x)−1 = (g −1 , g · x),                                        (17)
and composition
                                              (g ′ , g · x) ◦ (g, x) = (g ′ g, x).                                 (18)
This is the groupoid avatar of an ordinary global symmetry action on a manifold M .


                                                               6
Example 2.4 (Atiyah groupoid). Let π : P → M be a right principal H-bundle. The Atiyah groupoid (sometimes
called gauge groupoid) of P is the Lie groupoid

                                            Atiyah(P ) := (P × P )/H ⇒ M,                                            (19)

whose arrows are classes
                                            [px , py ],       px ∈ Px , py ∈ Py ,                                    (20)
under the diagonal right action
                                       (px , py ) · k := (px · k, py · k),         k ∈ H.                            (21)
The source and target maps are
                                          s([px , py ]) = y,         t([px , py ]) = x,                              (22)
the unit at x is
                                                          1x = [px , px ],                                           (23)
the inverse is
                                                   [px , py ]−1 = [py , px ],                                        (24)
and the composition law is
                                               [pz , py ] ◦ [py , px ] = [pz , px ].                                 (25)
   Equivalently, an arrow of Atiyah(P ) may be described as a H-equivariant map

                                                      αyx : Px → Py .                                                (26)

Indeed, given the class [py , px ], we define the map αyx : Px → Py as the assignment αyx (p) = q, with p = px h,
and q = py h, for all p ∈ Px . This map is well defined because if we change the representative [p′y , p′x ] = [py , px ],
then there is k ∈ H such that p′x = px k and p′y = py k. Then given p ∈ Px , p = px h = px kk −1 h = p′x (k −1 h), and
  ′
αyx (p) = p′y (k −1 h) = py h = q.
    The two examples above are the basic geometric models that will reappear throughout the paper. Action groupoids
encode ordinary global symmetries, while Atiyah groupoids encode local frame changes associated with a principal
bundle even in the absence of a nontrivial global symmetry group. A useful concept we will use in the context of
Atiyah groupoids is that of bisections.
Definition 2.5. A bisection of a Lie groupoid Γ ⇒ M is a section b : M → Γ such that s ◦ b = idM and
φb := t ◦ b ∈ Diff(M ). The set of bisections forms a group [38] denoted Bis(Γ) that acts in a natural way on M by
means of (b, x) 7→ φb (x) .
Remark 2.6. For the Atiyah groupoid of a principal bundle π : P → M , Bis(Atiyah(P )) ∼    = Aut(P ) (the group of
bundle automorphisms φ : P → P ), and the subgroup Bis1 (Atiyah(P )) of bisections b covering the identity, that is
φb = idM , is isomorphic to the subgroup of gauge transformations Gau(P ), that is the subgroup of Aut(P ) covering
the identity.
Remark 2.7. For the action groupoid Γ = G × M corresponding to the action of the Lie group G on a smooth
manifold M , Example 2.3, the group G is a canonical subgroup of the group of bisections Bis(G × M ). Indeed, for
any g ∈ G we define the bisection bg (x) = (g, x), for all x ∈ M . Then, clearly, φbg (x) = gx, and the original action
of G on M is recovered as the action of the subgroup G of the group of bisections.

2.2    The Poincaré groupoid of a Lorentzian spacetime
Let (M, g) be a d-dimensional Lorentzian spacetime. To simplify notation, we shall assume that (M, g) is oriented
and time-oriented, so that its bundle of oriented orthochronous orthonormal frames
                                                       ↑
                                                      O+ (M, g) → M                                                  (27)
                 ↑
is a principal SO+ (1, d − 1)-bundle. All constructions below admit an obvious variant using the full orthonormal
frame bundle and the full Lorentz group.


                                                                 7
Definition 2.8 ([26]). The Poincaré groupoid of (M, g) is the gauge groupoid
                                              ↑                      ↑
                               Poin(M, g) := O+ (M, g) ×SO↑ (1,d−1) O+ (M, g) ⇒ M.                                    (28)
                                                                    +


   There is an equivalent intrinsic description which is often more convenient.
Proposition 2.9 ([26]). The Poincaré groupoid Poin(M, g) is canonically isomorphic to the Lie groupoid whose
arrows are triples
                                                (y, Tyx , x),                                           (29)
where x, y ∈ M and
                                                    Tyx : Tx M → Ty M                                                 (30)
is an orientation- and time-orientation-preserving linear isometry, i.e.
                                                          ∗
                                                         Tyx gy = gx .                                                (31)

and Tyx maps the future-component of the light cone at x into the future-component of the light cone at y. The
source, target, identity, inverse and composition maps are given by

                                           s(y, Tyx , x) = x,       t(y, Tyx , x) = y,                                (32)

                                                     1x = (x, 1Tx M , x),                                             (33)
                                                (y, Tyx , x)−1 = (x, Tyx
                                                                      −1
                                                                         , y),                                        (34)
and
                                       (z, Tzy , y) ◦ (y, Tyx , x) = (z, Tzy ◦ Tyx , x).                              (35)
                         ↑                      ↑
Proof. Let [uy , ux ] ∈ O+ (M, g) ×SO↑ (1,d−1) O+ (M, g), where
                                       +


                                    ux : R1,d−1 → Tx M,             uy : R1,d−1 → Ty M                                (36)

are oriented orthochronous orthonormal frames. The map

                                           [uy , ux ] 7−→ uy ◦ u−1
                                                                x : Tx M → Ty M                                       (37)
                                                                                  ↑
is a well-defined linear isometry: replacing (uy , ux ) by (uy h, ux h) for h ∈ SO+ (1, d−1) gives uy h◦(ux h)−1 = uy ◦u−1
                                                                                                                        x ,
so the map depends only on the equivalence class. Conversely, given a linear isometry Tyx : Tx M → Ty M and any
frame ux at x, the frame
                                                         uy := Tyx ◦ ux                                                (38)
is orthonormal at y, and the class [uy , ux ] depends only on Tyx . The remaining structure maps are immediate.
Remark 2.10. No connection is required in Definition 2.8. The Levi-Civita connection determines a distinguished
subgroupoid, namely the holonomy groupoid obtained by parallel transport along curves, but for the purposes of
quantum reference frames we shall work with the full Poincaré groupoid. This reflects the fact that the kinematical
comparison of local frames is more primitive than any particular dynamical rule selecting preferred transports.
Proposition 2.11 ([26]). The Lie groupoid Poin(M, g) ⇒ M is transitive. For every x ∈ M, the isotropy group
                                ↑
Poin(M, g)xx is isomorphic to SO+ (1, d − 1) after choosing an oriented orthochronous orthonormal frame at x.
Proof. For any x, y ∈ M, the tangent spaces (Tx M, gx ) and (Ty M, gy ) are isometric Lorentzian vector spaces of
the same signature, hence there exists a linear isometry Tyx : Tx M → Ty M. This gives an arrow x → y, so the
groupoid is transitive. When x = y, the arrows are precisely the linear isometries of (Tx M, gx ) preserving the chosen
                                                                  ↑
orientation and time-orientation, hence the isotropy group is SO+   (1, d − 1).
    Thus Poin(M, g) should be viewed as the curved-spacetime analogue of the usual Poincaré symmetry group: its
arrows compare orthonormal frames at different points, while its isotropy groups recover the local Lorentz symmetry.
This groupoid coincides with the kinematical groupoid R(M) introduced in [27] in the context of the groupoidal
picture of quantum mechanics.


                                                                8
Remark 2.12. For Poin(M, g), bisections are pairs (φ, T ) with φ ∈ Diff(M) and T : T M → T M is a metric-
preserving bundle map over φ.
Definition 2.13. The subgroup of isometry-induced bisections of the Poincaré groupoid is
                                 n                                                                  o
          BisIsom (Poin(M, g)) := bφ ∈ Bis(Poin(M, g)) | φ ∈ Isom↑+ (M, g), bφ (x) = (φ(x), dφx , x) .         (39)

This group is distinct from the isotropy group Poin(M, g)xx , which consists of Lorentz transformations based at one
fixed point.

2.3     Flat spacetime and recovery of the ordinary Poincaré picture
We now highlight that in Minkowski spacetime the Poincaré groupoid collapses to the familiar action-groupoid
description.
Example 2.14 (Minkowski spacetime). Let (M, η) be d-dimensional Minkowski spacetime. Since T M is globally
trivial,
                                          TM ∼ = M × R1,d−1 ,                                        (40)
every arrow of Poin(M, η) may be written uniquely as
                                                                         ↑
                                    (y, Λ, x),      x, y ∈ M,      Λ ∈ SO+ (1, d − 1),                         (41)
where Λ acts as the corresponding Lorentz transformation in the global orthonormal frame. Hence
                                      Poin(M, η) ∼          ↑
                                                 = M × SO (1, d − 1) × M
                                                                   +                                           (42)
as a smooth manifold.
          ↑                  ↑
    Let P+   = T (1, d−1)⋊SO+  (1, d−1) be the proper orthochronous Poincaré group acting on Minkowski spacetime
by (a, Λ) · x = Λx + a. The associated action groupoid is
                                                       ↑
                                                      P+ ⋉ M ⇒ M.                                              (43)
Proposition 2.15. There is a canonical isomorphism of Lie groupoids
                                            Poin(M, η) ∼    ↑
                                                       = P ⋉ M.     +                                          (44)
Explicitly, the isomorphism is given by
                                                 ↑
                               Φ : Poin(M, η) → P+ ⋉ M,              (y, Λ, x) 7→ ((a, Λ), x),                 (45)
where
                                                       a := y − Λx.                                            (46)
Its inverse is
                                           Φ−1 ((a, Λ), x) = (Λx + a, Λ, x).                                   (47)
Proof. The formula for Φ is well defined because for fixed x, y, Λ there is a unique translation vector a satisfying
y = Λx + a. A direct computation shows that source and target maps are preserved:
                                  s((y, Λ, x)) = x,        t((y, Λ, x)) = y = (a, Λ) · x.                      (48)
For the composition, let a := y − Λx and a′ := z − Λ′ y. Then
             Φ (z, Λ′ , y) ◦ (y, Λ, x) = Φ(z, Λ′ Λ, x) = ((z − Λ′ Λx, Λ′ Λ), x) = ((a′ + Λ′ a, Λ′ Λ), x),      (49)
                                      

which is exactly the product
                                                 ((a′ , Λ′ ), y) ◦ ((a, Λ), x)                                 (50)
in the action groupoid. The formula for the inverse is immediate.
                                          ↑
    Because of 2.7, the Poincaré group P+    is now a subgroup of the group of bisections of the Poincaré groupoid
Poin(M, η), whose action on Minkowski spacetime M is induced from the action of the group of bisections on
M recovering the complete action-groupoid structure of Minkowski spacetime from its Poincaré groupoid, hence
Prop. 2.15 is the geometric core of the flat-spacetime consistency check stated in Sec. 1: once the operational
formalism is developed for action groupoids, the ordinary Poincaré-group description is recovered automatically from
the groupoid picture.


                                                              9
2.4    The Wigner groupoid
Besides the Poincaré groupoid, there is a closely related phase-space groupoid which will be useful when discussing
particle sectors and, later on, relational quantum fields.
Definition 2.16. Let (M, g) be a Lorentzian spacetime. The Wigner groupoid of (M, g) is the Lie groupoid
                                                     Wig(M, g) ⇒ T ∗ M                                                  (51)
whose arrows are triples
                                                          (py , Tyx , px ),                                             (52)
where px ∈ Tx∗ M , py ∈ Ty∗ M , and Tyx : Tx M → Ty M is a linear isometry satisfying the compatibility condition
                                                           ∗
                                                          Tyx py = px ,                                                 (53)
where Tyx
       ∗
          : Ty∗ M → Tx∗ M denotes the dual (pullback) map. The source and target maps are
                                 s(py , Tyx , px ) = (x, px ),        t(py , Tyx , px ) = (y, py ),                     (54)
the identities are
                                                  1(x,px ) = (px , 1Tx M , px ),                                        (55)
and the composition law is
                                    (pz , Tzy , py ) ◦ (py , Tyx , px ) = (pz , Tzy ◦ Tyx , px ).                       (56)
     Intuitively, Wig(M, g) refines Poin(M, g) by keeping track not only of local frames but also of cotangent vectors,
i.e. momenta. It is therefore the natural groupoid analogue of the usual Wigner picture of particle kinematics.
     For the orbit list below we assume d ≥ 3. In 1 + 1 dimensions the future and past null shells each split into two
orbits (right- and left-moving rays), and every spacelike shell splits into two orbits distinguished by the sign of the
spatial component. The massive future and past shells remain single orbits.
Proposition 2.17 ([26]). Let dim(M) ≥ 3, then the orbits of Wig(M, g) in T ∗ M are the mass shells determined
by the Lorentzian norm of the covector and, if a time-orientation is fixed, by its time orientation. More precisely, the
standard orbits are:
                           Om,+ = {(x, p) ∈ T ∗ M | gx−1 (p, p) = m2 > 0, p future-directed},
                            O0,+ = {(x, p) ∈ T ∗ M | gx−1 (p, p) = 0, p future-directed},
                              O0 = {(x, 0) ∈ T ∗ M},
                         Otach,m = {(x, p) ∈ T ∗ M | gx−1 (p, p) = −m2 < 0}.
There are analogous past-directed components for the massive and massless sectors.
Proof. An arrow (py , Tyx , px ) exists precisely when px = Tyx∗
                                                                 py . Since Tyx is a Lorentz isometry, it preserves the value
of g (p, p) and, in the oriented/time-oriented setting, also preserves the future/past decomposition. Conversely,
     −1

suppose (x, px ) and (y, py ) have the same Lorentzian norm and the same time orientation. By Proposition 2.11, there
exists at least one Lorentz isometry T0 : Tx M → Ty M. The covectors T0∗ py and px then lie in the same mass shell of
(Tx M, gx ). Since SO+  ↑
                          (1, d − 1) acts transitively on each mass shell, there exists Λ ∈ SO+ ↑
                                                                                                  (1, d − 1) ∼
                                                                                                             = Poin(M, g)xx
with Λ T0 py = px . Setting Tyx := T0 ◦ Λ produces the required arrow.
        ∗ ∗


Remark 2.18. The orbit decomposition of Wig(M, g) is the groupoid counterpart of the familiar classification of
one-particle kinematics by mass shell [39]. It is therefore natural to expect that irreducible or projective representations
of appropriate orbit restrictions of Wig(M, g) will capture the local particle sectors of the theory (see [26]). We will
not need the full representation-theoretic classification in the present paper, but this picture helps motivate the role
of the Wigner groupoid in the background geometry of the theory.
Example 2.19 (Flat spacetime). In Minkowski spacetime, the trivialization of T ∗ M identifies Wig(M, η) with the
action groupoid of the proper orthochronous Poincaré group acting on phase space by the cotangent-lifted affine
action:
                                      (a, Λ) · (x, p) = Λx + a, (Λ−1 )∗ p .                                 (57)
                                                                         

Thus, in the flat case, the Wigner groupoid reduces to the standard global phase-space kinematics.


                                                                 10
2.5    Hilbert bundles and unitary representations of Lie groupoids
To quantize a groupoid kinematics one must replace the usual notion of a unitary group representation on a single
Hilbert space by a representation on a field of Hilbert spaces over the object manifold.
Definition 2.20. A measurable field of Hilbert spaces over a standard Borel space X is a family
                                                              π
                                                     G
                                              H =        Hx −−→ X                                                   (58)
                                                        x∈X

of separable Hilbert spaces together with a distinguished set of measurable sections, ψ : x 7→ ψ(x) ∈ Hx , satisfying
the usual axioms of direct-integral theory (see, for instance, [40, 41]). The associated direct-integral Hilbert space is
denoted                                                 Z ⊕
                                                  H=        Hx dµ(x),                                               (59)
                                                          X
for any chosen σ-finite measure µ on X.
    When continuous or smooth structure is relevant, one may work with continuous or smooth Hilbert bundles,
respectively. In the following, we shall indeed work with continuous groupoids, although most examples of interest in
this paper will be with respect to Lie groupoids.
Definition 2.21. Let M be a manifold, Γ ⇒ M be a measurable groupoid and let H = x∈M Hx → M be a
                                                                                              F
measurable field of Hilbert spaces over M . A unitary representation of Γ on H is a measurable assignment
                                       α:x→y∈Γ            7−→      Uα : Hx → Hy                                     (60)
such that:
   1. each Uα is unitary;
   2. for every composable pair (α, β) ∈ Γ(2) ,
                                                        Uα◦β = Uα Uβ ;                                              (61)
   3. for every x ∈ M ,
                                                          U1x = 1Hx .                                               (62)
    This is simply a functor U : Γ → Hilbert, from the groupoid Γ (viewed as a small category) to the category Hilbert
of Hilbert spaces and bounded operators, together with the appropriate measurability condition (see [26] for details).
Definition 2.22. Let PH = x∈M P(Hx ) be the associated field of projective Hilbert spaces. A projective repre-
                                F
sentation of Γ on H is a measurable assignment
                                      α:x→y         7−→       φα : P(Hx ) → P(Hy )                                  (63)
such that φα preserves probability amplitudes and
                                         φα◦β = φα ◦ φβ ,          φ1x = 1P(Hx ) .                                  (64)
Proposition 2.23. Every unitary representation U of Γ on H induces a projective representation PU on PH by
                                                  (PU )α ([ψ]) = [Uα ψ].                                            (65)
Proof. This is immediate from the functoriality and unitarity of U .
    For the purposes of this paper, honest unitary representations will be the primary object. Projective representations
are nevertheless important for physical reasons: they arise naturally from Wigner symmetry principles and, in many
cases, can be encoded by central U (1)-extensions or multiplier cocycles of the underlying groupoid.
Remark 2.24. The representation theory of the Poincaré and Wigner groupoids, including projective aspects and
orbitwise classification results, provides the conceptual background for interpreting the fibers Hx as local quantum
state spaces and the arrows Uα as coherent transport maps between them. In the present paper, however, we only
require the functorial unitary framework of Definition 2.21; the more refined classification theory will be invoked only
heuristically.

2.6           Continuous groupoids as measure groupoids
Let Γ ⇒ M be a second countable Hausdorff continuous groupoid with source and target maps

                                                           s, t : Γ → M.                                                            (66)

We assume throughout that Γ is endowed with a continuous left Haar system2

                                                          ν = {ν x }x∈M ,                                                           (67)

that is, for each x ∈ M , ν x : Bor(Γx ) → [0, ∞] is a positive Radon measure on the target fiber, with support

                                                          supp ν x = Γx ,                                                           (68)

depending continuously on x, and satisfying the left-invariance property
                          Z                             Z
                                f (α ◦ β) dν s(α) (β) =       f (γ) dν t(α) (γ),                  α ∈ Γ,                            (69)
                                  Γs(α)                             Γt(α)

for every f ∈ Cc (Γ) (also written as α ◦ ν s(α) = ν t(α) ). This is shown pictorially in Fig. 2.
    Let µ be a σ-finite quasi-invariant Radon measure on the base M . Then ν and µ determine a measure λ on Γ by
                          Z                   Z Z                     
                              f (α) dλ(α) :=            f (α) dν x (α) dµ(x),        f ∈ Cc (Γ).            (70)
                              Γ                     M     Γx

Quasi-invariance means that λ is mutually absolutely continuous with its push-forward under inversion. Equivalently,
there exists a measurable modular function
                                                   δ : Γ → R>0                                                 (71)
such that
                                            dλ−1
                                                  (α) = δ(α)−1 ,                                                                    (72)
                                             dλ
where λ−1 denotes the image measure of λ under α 7→ α−1 , ι∗ λ = λ−1 .
Definition 2.25. The data (Γ, ν, δ, µ, λ) will be called the measure groupoid associated with (Γ ⇒ M, ν, µ).
   2 This is an extra assumption for general continuous groupoids, though for Lie groupoids a smooth Haar system exists if Γ and M are

second countable and Hausdorff [42].


                                                                    12
    Associated with the measure groupoid is the Hilbert space L2 (Γ, λ) and the corresponding groupoid von Neumann
algebra Vλ (Γ) obtained from the left-regular convolution representation (see [27–29] for the general construction).
We shall not need the full construction here, but only the fact that it exists canonically once the measure-groupoid
data are fixed.
Remark 2.26. The measure-groupoid formalism provides a natural candidate for the ambient algebra of observables
associated with the kinematics of Γ. In the present paper we work instead with operator fields on direct-integral
Hilbert spaces, since this is the most transparent setting in which to formulate the operational quantum reference
frame formalism. The two viewpoints are expected to be closely related; in the case of action groupoids the groupoid
von Neumann algebra reduces to the usual crossed product [43].
Definition 2.27. Let (Γ ⇒ M, ν, µ) be a second countable Hausdorff continuous groupoid endowed with a continuous
left Haar system. Then ν is said to be source-compatible with µ if for µ-almost every x ∈ M ,

                                                            s∗ ν x ≪ µ                                         (73)

where s∗ ν x (N ) := ν x (s−1 (N ) ∩ Γx ).
Proposition 2.28. Let (Γ ⇒ M, ν, µ) be a transitive second countable Hausdorff continuous groupoid endowed
with a continuous left Haar system ν and suppose µ is a nonzero σ-finite quasi-invariant Radon measure. Then ν is
source-compatible with µ.
Proof. Let x, y ∈ M . By transitivity, there exists α : x → y. Left invariance of the Haar system gives

                                                         (Lα )∗ ν x = ν y ,                                    (74)

where Lα : Γs(α) → Γt(α) , Lα β = α ◦ β, while s ◦ Lα = s on Γx . Consequently,

                                         s∗ ν y = s∗ (Lα )∗ ν x = (s ◦ Lα )∗ ν x = s∗ ν x .                    (75)

Thus there exists a measure κ on M such that s∗ ν x = κ for every x ∈ M . Let N ∈ Bor(M ) satisfy µ(N ) = 0. Then
                                      Z                               Z
                        λ(t−1 (N )) =    ν x (t−1 (N ) ∩ Γx ) dµ(x) =   ν x (Γx ) dµ(x) = 0.                  (76)
                                             M                                     N

But
                                              λ−1 s−1 (N ) = λ t−1 (N ) = 0.                                   (77)
                                                                      

Mutual absolute continuity, and in particular λ ≪ λ applied to s (N ), therefore implies
                                                            −1                −1

                                                Z                      Z
                             0 = λ s−1 (N ) =      s∗ ν x (N ) dµ(x) =                                         (78)
                                            
                                                                         κ(N ) dµ(x).
                                                        M                          M

Since κ(N ) is a nonnegative constant and µ is nonzero, we obtain κ(N ) = 0. Thus κ ≪ µ.

Definition 2.33. Normal states of A(H ) can then be defined as positive decomposable ρ =                 M

Remark 2.34. Note that S (H ) ̸= D(H ) in general; in particular, D(H ) ⊂ A(H )∗ ̸⊂ B(H) in general while
S (H ) ⊂ A(H ) ⊂ B(H). Moreover, for ρ = (ρx ) ∈ D(H ), we have, for µ-almost every x ∈ M , ρx ≥ 0, but
if TrHx [ρx ] = 1 for µ-almost every x ∈ M then µ(M ) = 1. If µ(M ) = ∞ however (as is the case for the volume
measure on Minkowski for example), a constant field of fiber-states ρ = (ρx ) with each ρx ∈ D(Hx ) is not in
A(H )∗ .
Remark 2.35. We will interpret states in S (H ) as characterising preparation states (of the frame) while states in
D(H ) as characterising evaluation states (of the system) extracting probabilities from (relational local) observables.
   Continuous groupoids become interesting when we want to control the continuity of x 7→ Ax of operator fields.
Definition 2.36. Let π : H → M be a continuous field of separable Hilbert spaces. A bounded operator field
A ∈ A(H ) is called ultraweakly continuous if, for every trace-norm continuous section
                                                  G
                                      χ:M →           T (Hx ),     x 7→ χx ,                         (96)
                                                          x∈M

the scalar function x 7→ TrHx [χx Ax ] is continuous. We write A(H )uw for the space of such fields.
     Again, given HS ⊠ HR , we can similarly define A(HS ⊠ HR )uw as the space of ultraweakly continuous operator
fields in A(HS ⊠ HR ).
Definition 2.37. An operator field B = (Bx )x∈M ∈ A(HS ⊠ HR ) (resp. A(HS ⊠ HR )uw ) is called Γ-invariant if

                                          Wα Bs(α) Wα† = Bt(α)             for all α ∈ Γ.                                 (97)

The set of all such invariant fields will be denoted by A(HS ⊠ HR )Γ (resp. A(HS ⊠ HR )Γuw ).
   This is the groupoid analogue of the invariant algebra B(HS ⊗ HR )G in the group-based theory.
Definition 2.38. An operator field A = (Ax )x∈M ∈ A(H ) (resp. A(H )uw ) is called isotropy-invariant if

                                        Vγ Ax Vγ† = Ax            for every γ ∈ Γxx , x ∈ M.                              (98)

The set of isotropy-invariant fields is denoted by A(H )iso (resp. A(H )iso
                                                                        uw ).

    This is the groupoid analogue of the invariant algebra B(H)H for H a subgroup of G in the group-based theory
of homogeneous spaces G/H. The point of Definition 2.38 is that if A is isotropy-invariant and α, β : x → y, then

                                                     Vα Ax Vα† = Vβ Ax Vβ† .                                              (99)

Indeed, γ := β −1 ◦ α ∈ Γxx , so Vγ Ax Vγ† = Ax by isotropy-invariance, which gives Vα Ax Vα† = Vβ Vγ Ax Vγ† Vβ† =
Vβ Ax Vβ† . Thus such an operator may be transported along arrows without ambiguity, just as an H-invariant operator
on a homogeneous space G/H may be transported by the group action.

2.8    Bisections
Bisections can be used to define actions over the whole direct integral algebra, rather than fiber-wise, as follows.
Definition 2.39. Let (Γ ⇒ M, ν, µ) be a Lie groupoid, and let U : Γ ↷ H be a unitary representation of Γ with
     R⊕

                      (Ub Ψ)x :=                    (x)          Ub(φ−1 (x)) Ψ(φ−1        x

Definition 2.40. A Lie groupoid (Γ ⇒ M, ν, µ) is called bisection-admissible if (φb )∗ µ ∼ µ for all b ∈ Bis(Γ).

2.9     States and continuity
In the context of infinite-dimensional Hilbert spaces, topologies and different notions of continuity become important
to distinguish. Just as strong, weak and ultraweak continuity of (unitary) representations can be defined for group
representations, similar notions extend to groupoids.
    In [41], weak and strong continuity of representations of continuous groupoids were analysed. However, although
commonly discussed, the weak and strong topologies are not operational: they refer to convergence in inner products
and in vectors, respectively. Instead, one should look at notions of continuity which have to do with convergence in
probabilities, i.e. the ultraweak topology. To the best of our knowledge, this has not been discussed in the literature
in the context of continuous groupoids. Here, we provide a sufficient exposition for the ensuing discussion, but
encourage further work on the topic.
    The ultraweak topology is arguably the relevant one in operational (quantum) physics. A sequence of operators
(An ) in B(H) converge ultraweakly to A ∈ B(H) if Tr[ρAn ] → Tr[ρA] for all density operators ρ ∈ D(H ). That is,
ultraweak convergence is the convergence of expectation values.
    Ultraweak continuity has to do with probabilities when evaluated (through the trace) on elements of the predual
of the space of operators, i.e. typically T (H) as the predual of B(H). However, as we have seen, there are several
spaces of preduals to consider here.



                                                                     16
Remark 2.43. There are at least three possible notions of ultraweak continuity that one may wish to define: a global
one given by the topology σ(A(H ), A(H )∗ ), a fiber-wise one given by the topologies σ(B(Hx , Hy ), T (Hy , Hx ))
(i.e. linking different fibers together), and a fiber-wise one given by the topologies σ(B(Hx ), T (Hx )) (i.e. at the
isotropy group level).
Definition 2.44. Let π : H = x∈M Hx → M be a continuous field of separable Hilbert spaces over M and let
                                   F
(Γ, ν, δ, µ, λ) be the continuous measure groupoid associated with (Γ ⇒ M, ν, µ). Let U : Γ ↷ H be a unitary
representation on H . U is said to be
   1. fiber-wise ultraweakly continuous if for every normal predual test section χ,4 α 7→ TrHt(α) [Uα χα ] is continuous,5
   2. isotropy ultraweakly continuous if for every x ∈ M and every χ ∈ T (Hx ), the map Γxx ∋ γ 7→ TrHx [Uγ χ] is
      continuous,

   3. weakly continuous if for all continuous sections ξ, η : M → H , α 7→ Uα ξs(α) ηt(α) is continuous,

   4. strongly continuous if for all continuous sections ξ : M → H , α 7→ Uα ξs(α) is continuous.

Remark 2.45. Note that the arrow giving the unitary Uα ∈ B(Hs(α) , Ht(α) ) pairs with the trace class T (Ht(α) , Hs(α) ),
which reduces to the fiber predual T (Hx ) only on the isotropy diagonal. It is never an element of the global algebra
            R⊕
A(H ) = M B(Hx ) dµ for α off the isotropy diagonal.
Theorem 2.46. Let π : H = x∈M Hx → M be a continuous field of separable Hilbert spaces over M and let
                                 F
(Γ, ν, δ, µ, λ) be the measure groupoid associated with (Γ ⇒ M, ν, µ). Let U : Γ ↷ H be a unitary representation
on H . Then
   1. U is strongly continuous iff U is weakly continuous iff U is fiber-wise ultraweakly continuous,
   2. if U is fiber-wise ultraweakly continuous then it is isotropy ultraweakly continuous.
Proof.   1. It is known [41, Lemma 2.6] that weak and strong continuity of unitary representations are equivalent.
      Thus, it is sufficient to show their equivalence with fiber-wise ultraweak continuity of unitary representations.
       Assume first that U is fiber-wise ultraweakly continuous. For continuous sections ξ, η of H , define the rank-one
       normal predual test section
                                           χξ,η
                                             α := ξs(α)    ηt(α) ∈ T (Ht(α) , Hs(α) ).                             (106)

       Thus fiber-wise ultraweak continuity implies weak continuity.
       Conversely assume that U is weakly continuous. Let χ be a normal predual test section and fix α0 ∈ Γ. On

    2. Isotropy ultraweak continuity is just fiber-wise ultraweak continuity for the cases where s(α) = t(α).


Remark 2.47. Note that in the group case M = {∗}, fiber-wise and isotropy ultraweak continuity reduce to the
same notion of ultraweak continuity, and the three topologies discussed above reduce to the same one. In an action-
groupoid model of a torsor, isotropy continuity is vacuous (since stabilisers are trivial so isotropy groups are trivial),
while fiber-wise ultraweak continuity recovers the usual ultraweak continuity of the group representation.
    Thus, we can safely work interchangeably with weakly, strongly and fiber-wise ultraweakly continuous unitary
representations of continuous (in particular Lie) groupoids.
    In the rest of this paper, we will sometimes be interested in sets of operator fields (respectively, of states) that
form subsets of A(H ) (respectively, of S (H )). We thus use the following notation.
Notation 2.48. We write O ⊂ A(H ) for a set of measurable operator fields in A(H ) and S ⊂ S (H ) for a
(typically convex) set of measurable operator fields of states.
Outlook toward the operational formalism. The geometric picture developed above suggests the following replacement
of the standard symmetry-based quantum kinematics.
                         locally compact group action         ⇝   continuous groupoids,
                              homogeneous space               ⇝   principal bundle,
                      single Hilbert space representation     ⇝   Hilbert bundle,
                          unitary group representation        ⇝   unitary groupoid representation,
                            global covariant POVM             ⇝   fiber-wise covariant POVM.
   The first four entries have now been introduced. The fifth will be the starting point of the operational formalism
developed later. In particular, Sec. 3 recalls the usual group-based theory of operational quantum reference frames,
and Sec. 4 reformulates it for Lie groupoids. The flat-space reduction provided by Proposition 2.15 will then imply
that the groupoid formalism recovers the standard relativistic QRF framework on Minkowski spacetime.


3     Operational quantum reference frames for locally compact groups
In this section we recall the operational formulation of quantum reference frames for locally compact groups [7,
19, 21], in the form needed later to compare it with the Lie-groupoid framework. We keep only those parts of the
formalism that are essential for the passage from groups to action groupoids: systems of covariance, relativization
maps, relative observables and states, and the localization limit.
    Throughout this section, G denotes a locally compact second countable Hausdorff topological group and Σ a
principal homogeneous left G-space, i.e. Σ ∼= G.

3.1     Systems, invariance and relativization
Let S be a quantum system carrying an ultraweakly continuous unitary representation
                                                   US : G → U (HS ).                                                (112)
The compound system “system + frame” is described on the tensor product Hilbert space HS ⊗ HR with diagonal
representation US (g) ⊗ UR (g). The algebra of invariant bounded operators is
             B(HS ⊗ HR )G := {A ∈ B(HS ⊗ HR ) | US (g) ⊗ UR (g)AUS (g)† ⊗ UR (g)† = A, ∀g ∈ G}.                     (113)


                                                             18
Relativization for locally compact groups. We first focus on the notion of relativization on groups themselves.
We begin with the well-established [7, 19] definition for operational quantum reference frames.
Definition 3.1. A quantum reference frame on the locally compact second countable topological group G is a triple
R = (UR , ER , HR ), where:

   1. HR is a separable complex Hilbert space;
   2. UR : G → U (HR ) is an ultraweakly continuous unitary representation;
   3. ER : Bor(G) → E(HR ) is a normalized POVM satisfying the covariance condition

                               UR (g) ER (∆) UR (g)† = ER (g · ∆),          g ∈ G, ∆ ∈ Bor(G).                 (114)

The POVM ER will be called the frame observable.
Remark 3.2. In much of the QRF literature one allows projective unitary representations. For the purposes of the
present paper, and in order to match the groupoid definitions of Sec. 1, we work with honest unitary representations.
The projective variant can be incorporated later by passing to suitable central extensions or multiplier cocycles.

Definition 3.3. Let R = (UR , ER , HR ) be a quantum reference frame on Σ = G, and let S be a system with
representation US . The associated relativization map is

                                             ¥R : B(HS ) → B(HS ⊗ HR ),                                        (115)

defined by the operator-valued integral [46]
                                                   Z
                                       ¥R (A) :=           US (g)AUS (g)† ⊗ dER (g).                           (116)
                                                       G

                                        uw
Remark 3.4. Note that ¥R (B(HS ))         ⊂ B(HS ⊗ HR )G . Thus relative observables are not merely invariant:
they are those invariant observables on the compound system that arise from the chosen frame observable ER via
relativization.
   A particularly useful special case arises when one conditions on a chosen state of the frame.

Definition 3.5. Let ω ∈ D(HR ) be a state (density operator) of the frame. The restriction map

                                               Γω : B(HS ⊗ HR ) → B(HS )                                       (117)

is defined by
                         Tr[ρ Γω (B)] = Tr[(ρ ⊗ ω)B],            ρ ∈ D(HS ), B ∈ B(HS ⊗ HR ).                  (118)
This gives rise to the restricted relativization map

                              ¥R          R
                               ω := Γω ◦ ¥ : B(HS ) → B(HS )                                                   (119)
