Relativization for torsors of locally compact groups. We now examine relativization for torsors (i.e. principal
homogeneous spaces) of locally compact groups. Any space Σ with a continuous transitive G-action α : G × Σ → Σ
can be written as G/Hx , where Hx is the stabiliser of some point x ∈ Σ. This holds even in the case of principal
homogeneous spaces i.e. for G-torsors, where Hx = {e}; colloquially, we might say that a G-torsor is like a group
“+ a point". Indeed, the equality Σ = G does not hold anymore, although Σ ∼= G.


                                                               19
Remark 3.6. Note that for any normalized G-covariant POVM E : Bor(Σ) → E(H) on a homogeneous G-space Σ,
supp E = Σ. This is because G acts transitively on Σ.
Definition 3.7. Let G be a locally compact second countable topological group, Σ be a principal G-space and o ∈ Σ.
A principal6 quantum reference frame on Σ is a tuple Ro = (URo , ERo , HRo ) where
   1. HRo is a separable complex Hilbert space;
   2. URo : G → U (HRo ) is an ultraweakly continuous unitary representation;
   3. ERo : Bor(Σ) → E(HRo ) is a normalized POVM satisfying the covariance condition
                                 URo (g) ERo (∆) URo (g)† = ERo (g · ∆),                 g ∈ G, ∆ ∈ Bor(Σ).      (122)

The POVM ERo will be called the frame observable.
Definition 3.8. Let Ro = (URo , ERo , HRo ) be a principal quantum reference frame on Σ ∼
                                                                                        = G, and let S be a
system with representation US . For p ∈ Σ, let gp ∈ G be the unique element with p = gp · o. The associated
relativization map is
                                       ¥Ro : B(HS ) → B(HS ⊗ HR )G ,                                  (123)
defined by the operator-valued integral [46]
                                                         Z
                                          ¥Ro (A) :=           US (gp )AUS (gp )† ⊗ dERo (p).                    (124)
                                                           Σ

Given ω ∈ D(HRo ), the restricted relativization map is given by
                                ¥R
                                 ω := Γω ◦ ¥
                                   o         Ro
                                                : B(HS ) → B(HS )                                                (125)
                                                           Z
                                                                                  E
                                                     A 7→    US (gp )AUS (gp )† dµωRo (p),                       (126)
                                                                          Σ

Remark 3.9. For most purposes, we can, at least in the principal case considered here, drop the o ∈ Σ in the above.
We only included it here to contrast the locally compact group case and the homogeneous space case, to highlight
that there is here a choice of o ∈ Σ that is usually implicit, and to mirror with the construction of quantum reference
frames for groupoids of Sec. 4 where such considerations do become important. Indeed, a groupoid reduces to a
group when M = {∗}, but a group action on a principal homogeneous space is instead an action groupoid, which is
different. We can however consider the case where Σ = G and where Σ ∼        = G without distinction for now.
   As mentioned, this construction can be generalised beyond torsors [21, 46] to e.g. all homogeneous G-spaces,
though we are interested in the principal ones here.
Definition 3.10. Let R be a quantum reference frame on Σ.
   1. R is called sharp7 if ER is projection-valued.
   2. R is called localizable if ER has the norm-1 property, i.e.
                                                                  ∥ER (O)∥ = 1                                   (127)
      for every O ∈ Bor(Σ) such that ER (O) ̸= 0.
   We will come back to the localizability property in Sec. 3.2. In this paper, we will focus on principal QRFs.
Proposition 3.11 ([19]). The relativization map ¥R is linear, normal, unital and completely positive. If R is sharp,
then ¥R is multiplicative, hence a normal ∗-homomorphism. If R is principal and localizable, then ¥R is injective.
Remark 3.12. There is a dual picture of relativization that applies to states rather than operators [19]. We shall
not expand on this viewpoint in this paper.
Remark 3.13. Note that for either the locally compact group or the torsor case, G-invariant operators are invariant
under relativization: if A ∈ B(HS )G then ¥R (A) = A ⊗ 1B(HR ) and for all ω ∈ D(HR ), ¥R ω (A) = A.
  6 Since Σ is principal, such quantum reference frames are called principal.
  7 In particular, if Σ is principal, these quantum reference frames are called ideal.




                                                                     20
3.2    Localization limit and recovery of the absolute description
The localizability property is operationally crucial: it guarantees the existence of frame states producing probability
measures that can be concentrated around arbitrary frame values with arbitrarily high precision [15, 19].
Proposition 3.14 ([47]). Let Σ be a principal homogeneous G-space and E : Bor(Σ) → E(H) be a normalized
POVM. The following are equivalent:

   1. E has the norm-1 property.
   2. For every X ∈ Bor(Σ) for which E(X) ̸= 0, there is a sequence of unit vectors |ϕn ⟩ ⊂ H for which

                                                   lim ⟨ϕn |E(X)|ϕn ⟩ = 1 .                                         (128)
                                                  n→∞


   3. (ϵ-decidability property) For every X ∈ Bor(Σ) such that E(X) ̸= 0 and for any ϵ > 0 there exists a unit vector
      |ϕϵ ⟩ ∈ H for which
                                                  ⟨ϕϵ |E(X)|ϕϵ ⟩ > 1 − ϵ .                                      (129)

   An important consequence is the following.
Corollary 3.15 ([19]). If a covariant normalized POVM E : Bor(Σ) → E(H) has the norm-1 property and Σ is
metrizable, then for any x ∈ Σ there exists a sequence of pure states (ωn ) ⊂ D(HR ) such that the sequence of
probability measures (µEωn ) converges weakly to the Dirac measure δx .

    We call such a sequence (ωn ) ⊂ D(HR ) a localizing sequence centred at the identity. The physical meaning
of this for QRFs is that, once the frame is prepared in states increasingly localized around a classical frame value,
the relative description converges to the ordinary non-relational one. We review this result in the case of localizable
QRFs on principal homogeneous spaces.
Theorem 3.16 (Fundamental theorem of operational quantum reference frames [19]). Let R = (UR , ER , HR ) be a
localizable principal frame, and let (ωn ) ⊂ D(HR ) be a localizing sequence centred at the identity. Then for every
A ∈ B(HS ),
                                                 lim ¥R ωn (A) = A                                             (130)
                                                   n→∞

in the ultraweak topology.
   In this operational sense, the ordinary kinematics of the system is recovered as a localization limit of the relational
description. This result is the reason that the standard, apparently non-relational description of a quantum system
may be understood as an effective description relative to a sufficiently good external frame.

3.3    The continuity of frame observables
The notion of localizability comes into contact with various notions of continuity of the frame observables, as we
now explore. Let F : Bor(M) → E(H) be any normalized POVM over Minkowski and V0 ⊂ M be a bounded region
of Minkowski spacetime. Hegerfeldt showed [48] that if the generator H of time translations is bounded from below
(i.e. the spectrum condition holds) and if ∃ρ ∈ D(H) such that Tr[ρF(V0 )] = 1 then either Tr[ρ · tF(V0 )] = 1 for all
t ∈ R or Tr[ρ · tF(V0 )] < 1 for almost every t ∈ R (the set of such t’s is dense and open). For normalized covariant
POVMs this is particularly problematic and, in Hegerfeldt’s words [48],
      “If at t = 0 a particle is strictly localized in a bounded region V0 then, unless it remains in V0 for all
      times, it cannot be strictly localized in a bounded region V, however large, for any finite time interval
      thereafter, and the particle localization immediately develops infinite ‘tails’. The spreading is over all
      space except possibly for ‘holes’ which, if any, will persist for all times [· · · ].”
    A weaker requirement is that ∥F(V0 )∥ = 1, i.e. that the localisation is arbitrarily well approximated to be in
V0 . This is the meaning of the norm-1 or localisability property of POVMs and QRFs. We link these notions of
localizability to various continuity conditions of the POVMs.



                                                           21
Definition 3.17. Let Σ be a real finite-dimensional manifold, E : Bor(Σ) → E(H) be a POVM and µ : Bor(Σ) → R
a regular measure. E is said to be absolutely continuous with respect to µ, written E ≪ac µ, if there exists a number
c ∈ R such that
                                        ∥E(∆)∥ ≤ cµ(∆)         ∀∆ ∈ Bor(Σ) .                                     (131)
Theorem 3.18. Let Σ be a topological space as above, µ : Bor(Σ) → R a regular non-atomic measure and
E : Bor(Σ) → E(H) be a POVM. If E ≪ac µ, then E does not have the norm-1 property.
Proof. Beneduci and Schroeck showed ([49], Thm 2.10) that such an E can only have the norm-1 property if
∥E({x})∥ ̸= 0 for each x ∈ Σ such that µ({x}) < ∞. But if µ is non-atomic then µ({x}) = 0 < ∞ for all x ∈ Σ and
so if E ≪ac µ then ∥E({x})∥ ≤ c · 0 = 0 ⇒ E({x}) = 0 which shows that E cannot have the norm-1 property.
    In particular, the Lebesgue measure on Minkowski is non-atomic; thus, any absolutely continuous POVM with
respect to that measure is not localizable. This covers POVMs which Rhave a bounded operator-valued density with
respect to the Lebesgue measure, i.e. POVMs of the form E(∆) = ∆ K(x)dµ(x) where K(x) ∈ B(H); and in
particular coherent-state POVMs [49]. A weaker notion to absolute continuity is that of µ-continuity, which we now
cover.
Definition 3.19. Let Σ be a real finite-dimensional manifold, E : Bor(Σ) → E(H) be a POVM and µ : Bor(Σ) → C
be a real measure. We say that E is µ-continuous, written E ≪ µ, if µ(∆) = 0 ⇒ E(∆) = 0 for all ∆ ∈ Bor(Σ).
    Note that if E ≪ac µ then E ≪ µ. Indeed, if ∃c < ∞ such that ∥E(∆)∥ ≤ cµ(∆) for all ∆ ∈ Bor(Σ), then
µ(∆) = 0 ⇒ ∥E(∆)∥ ≤ c · 0 = 0 ⇒ E(∆) = 0. The converse, however, need not hold: for example, the position
PVM on the real line is µ-continuous for µ the Lebesgue measure but not absolutely continuous with respect to µ,
since ∥E((0, ϵ))∥ = 1 for any ϵ > 0 while µ((0, ϵ)) = ϵ, so we cannot have 1 ≤ cϵ for finite c. We now show that
covariant POVMs are necessarily µ-continuous with respect to (quasi-)invariant measures.
Theorem 3.20. Let G be a locally compact, second countable Hausdorff topological group which acts transitively
on Σ ∼= G/H, a locally compact second countable topological space with Borel σ-algebra Bor(Σ), where H is a
closed subgroup of G. Let µ be a quasi-invariant8 σ-finite positive Borel measure on Σ. If E : Bor(Σ) → E(H) is
                                                                        dµEχ
a G-covariant normalized POVM then E ≪ µ, and for all χ ∈ T (H), ∃           ∈ L1 (Σ; µ). Moreover, if χ ≥ 0 then
                                                                         dµ
dµEχ                          R dµEχ
     ≥ 0 and if Tr[χ] = 1 then Σ     dµ = 1.
 dµ                              dµ
Proof. See App. A.1.
    Thus, covariant POVMs on homogeneous space have Radon-Nikodym derivatives in L1 . We now show that if
these covariant POVMs are absolutely continuous, then their Radon-Nikodym derivatives are in L1 ∩ L∞ – a property
which makes these densities nicer in the sense that any function in L1 ∩ L∞ is also in every other Lp space by
interpolation.
Theorem 3.21. Let Σ be a locally compact second countable topological space with Borel σ-algebra Bor(Σ), and
                                                                                       dµEχ
let µ be a σ-finite positive Borel measure on Σ. Then E ≪ac µ iff for all χ ∈ T (H), ∃      ∈ L∞ (Σ; µ). Moreover,
                                                                                        dµ
               dµEχ                           R dµEχ
if χ ≥ 0 then       ≥ 0 and if Tr[χ] = 1 then Σ      dµ = 1.
                dµ                                dµ
Proof. See App. A.2.
    Theorems 3.18 and 3.21 give a dichotomy. Covariance implies only µ-continuity, and hence the existence of L1
Radon-Nikodym densities for all Born measures. By contrast, absolute continuity E ≪ac µ is equivalent to essential
boundedness of all scalar Radon-Nikodym derivatives; the Banach-Steinhaus argument in App. A.2 then produces a
single constant c < ∞ satisfying ∥E(∆)∥ ≤ cµ(∆). For a non-atomic measure this is incompatible with the norm-1
property. Consequently, under Definition 3.18, there are no localizable absolutely continuous POVMs. Localizable
covariant POVMs may nevertheless be µ-continuous; along a localizing sequence their L1 densities must become
increasingly concentrated and cannot remain uniformly bounded in L∞ .
  8 A measure µ on Σ is quasi-invariant if ∀g ∈ G, µ(g · X) = 0 ⇔ µ(X) = 0 for all X ∈ Bor(Σ).




                                                               22
Remark 3.22. Several known examples of absolutely continuous covariant POVMs (e.g. coherent state POVMs
[49]) are not localizable.
    Beyond these tensions, Thm. 3.20 is useful for us in the context of relativization, since it guarantees the existence
                                           ER
of a Radon-Nikodym derivative fωER = dµdµ  ω
                                              ∈ L1 (Σ, µ), i.e. we can write the integral as a smearing with respect to
this function, that we call the frame smearing function [22], and the relevant quasi-invariant measure on the space.
It will also be useful for showing the µ-admissibility (see Sec. 4.1) of groupoid QRF in the recovery of torsor QRFs,
as we will see in Sec. 4.5.


4     Quantum reference frames for continuous groupoids
In this section we formulate the operational notion of a quantum reference frame for a continuous groupoid. The
guiding principle is simple: the group-based ingredients recalled in Sec. 3 are replaced by their groupoid analogues.
A single Hilbert space is replaced by a measurable field of Hilbert spaces, a unitary representation of a group by
a unitary representation of a continuous groupoid, and a covariant POVM on a homogeneous space by a family of
fiber-wise covariant POVMs on the target fibers of the groupoid.
    The analytic background for this replacement is provided by the measure-groupoid formalism and the associated
groupoid von Neumann algebra (see, for instance [27] and references therein). Conceptually, however, the operational
construction itself only needs three ingredients: a continuous groupoid, a measurable field of Hilbert spaces carrying
a unitary groupoid representation, and a covariant family of POVMs. The operator-algebraic structure associated
with the corresponding measure groupoid will become more important in Secs. 5, 6 and 7 where the framework is
applied to relational quantum field theory.

4.1     Groupoid quantum reference frames
We now formalize the operational notion of a quantum reference frame for a continuous groupoid.
Definition 4.1. Let Γ ⇒ M be a continuous groupoid. We say that a field of POVMs (Ex ) is measurable if for
every ∆ ∈ Bor(Γ), the field x 7→ Ex (∆ ∩ Γx ) is a measurable operator field.

Definition 4.2. A (principal) quantum reference frame for the continuous groupoid Γ ⇒ M based on Γ is a triple
R = (U, ER , HR ), where:
  1. HR = x∈M HR     x
                       → M is a continuous field of separable Hilbert spaces;
             F

    2. U is a fiber-wise ultraweakly continuous unitary representation of Γ on HR ;

    3. ER = {ExR }x∈M is a measurable field of normalized POVMs ExR : Bor(Γx ) → E(HR
                                                                                    x
                                                                                      ), satisfying the covariance
       condition
                                  s(α)             t(α)
                             Uα ER (∆) Uα† = ER (Lα ∆),          α ∈ Γ, ∆ ∈ Bor(Γs(α) ) ,                    (132)
       where Lα : Γs(α) → Γt(α) , β 7→ Lα (β) = α ◦ β.
The family ER is called the frame observable of R.
   The group-based formalism is recovered by replacing the family of fibers Γx by a single space Σ and fiber-wise
covariance under left translation in the groupoid by global covariance under G.

Remark 4.3. It is possible to define QRFs for groupoids based on spaces other than Γ. That is, the frame observables
need not take Borel subsets of Γx for x ∈ M , but Borel subsets of other spaces (perhaps Γx /Γxx , perhaps some
other groupoid spaces). Those generalise non-principal homogeneous space QRFs. One then expects the ensuing
construction of the relativization map to follow through assuming isotropy-invariant operators. We do not focus on
such QRFs in this paper, but these might be of interest if one wanted, for example, to define QRFs directly on the
                                                                  ↑
spacetime manifold M or on the space of 4-metrics GL(4, R)/SO+      (1, 3).




                                                           23
Remark 4.4. One may also want to generalise the notion of groupoid QRFs to measurable groupoids, and relax the
continuity assumptions of the field of Hilbert spaces and of the unitary representation. One challenge to overcome
this would be to define the countable additivity of POVMs
                                       ∞            ∞
                                              !
                                       G            X
                                 ERx
                                           ∆n =        ExR (∆n ),    ∆n ∈ Bor(Γx )                            (133)
                                           n=1           n=1

in some suitable topology; we leave this for future work.
Definition 4.5. Let R be a (principal) QRF for the continuous groupoid Γ ⇒ M . For µ-almost every x ∈ M and
πx : Γx → M be a measurable projection, we define

                                                 FxR,π : Bor(M ) → E(HR

                                                                        )                                                    (134)

and call (FxR,π ) the π-marginal frame POVM.9 In particular, if πx = s|Γx (i.e. πx−1 (N ) = s−1 (N ) ∩ Γx for all
N ∈ Bor(M )), we call FxR,s the source marginal of the frame observable.
    From there, we can also introduce several nice properties that the QRFs may or may not have.
Definition 4.6. Let R = (HR , U, ER ) be a quantum reference frame for the continuous groupoid Γ ⇒ M . R is
called

   1. µ-admissible if the source marginal of the frame observable does not see µ-null sets, i.e. given

                                        FxR,s (N ) := ExR (s−1 (N ) ∩ Γx ),       N ∈ Bor(M ),                               (136)

      we have that FxR,s is µ-continuous: FxR,s ≪ µ (i.e. µ(N ) = 0 ⇒ FxR,s (N ) = 0).

   2. Lie if Γ is a Lie groupoid.
   3. sharp if each ExR is projection-valued.
   4. principal if it is based on Γ and non-principal if not.

   5. ideal if it is both principal and sharp.
   6. localizable if each ExR has the norm-1 property, namely

                                                             ∥ExR (∆)∥ = 1                                                   (137)

      for every x ∈ M and every ∆ ∈ Bor(Γx ) with ExR (∆) ̸= 0.

   7. bisection-admissible if (Γ ⇒ M, ν, µ) is Lie and bisection-admissible.
    µ-admissibility will be useful for the independence of the relativization map defined below on the choice of
representative operator field.
Remark 4.7. If ExR is projection-valued then it has the norm-1 property. Hence, if R is sharp (i.e. ExR is projection-
valued for µ-almost every x) then it is localizable (i.e. ExR has the norm-1 property for µ-almost every x).

Definition 4.9. Let (Γ ⇒ M, ν, µ) be a Lie groupoid, and B ≤ Bis(Γ) be a subgroup of the group of bisections

(FxR,π ) B-compatible marginal frame observables.

                                                                25
4.2     Localization
Before discussing relativization, let us first discuss more general notions of localization of groupoid QRFs. Unlike in
the homogeneous space setting, where the normalization and covariance of the frame observables ensure that they
are supported over all of Σ, this can fail to hold in more general settings. Indeed, the covariance properties now relate
different ExR ’s. We thus untangle several notions of localization and supports.
Definition 4.13. Let R be a principal QRF for the continuous groupoid Γ ⇒ M .

   1. For µ-almost every x ∈ M , we write

Remark 4.14. Unlike the case of QRFs on homogeneous spaces, we need not have Locπ (R) = M in general.
This is because the groupoid covariance, in general, joins different ExR ’s and EyR ’s, rather than joining different
Borel subsets in the range of each ExR . Note however that the isotropy part of the covariance, i.e. γ ∈ Γxx giving
Uγ ExR (∆)Uγ† = ExR (Lγ ∆), does join different Borel subsets ∆ and Lγ ∆ for a fixed x; however, this is typically
not enough to ensure the full support on the space if the isotropy group is not transitive on Γx . Moreover, a
suitable subgroup B of the group of bisections may or may not help with this, depending on whether the (FxR,π ) are
B-compatible or not.
  10 Note that these definitions also work when M is not a spacetime manifold, but we will later use it in this sense.




                                                                   26
Remark 4.15. It can now be difficult to place a canonical notion of “localization" to a QRF, given it can be dependent
on a choice π: it is not always true that Locπ1 (R) = Locπ2 (R) for two families of projections π1 and π2 . There
does exist an intrinsic choice of π for Γ given by π = s|Γx , though one would expect the localization of the QRF
to change covariantly under (a subgroup B, e.g. in the case of the Poincaré groupoid over spacetime, the isometry
subgroup BisIsom (Poin(M, g)) of) bisections rather than stay invariant. Thus, perhaps it is more natural to choose
B-compatible π’s for suitable B; again, this is typically non-unique. Note however that supp R is not dependent on
any choice of π.
Remark 4.16. Different choices of π have different physical meanings and answer different questions related to
localization. For example, take πt = tΓx , then supp FxR,πt = {x} and Locπt (R, ω) = M for all ω ∈ S (HR ) and
Locπt (R) = M , i.e. the notions of localization from this πt answer a question of the form “where are the ontic frame
observables localized in spacetime" (and, indeed, they are localized µ-almost everywhere). Other choices, notably
those BisIsom (Poin(M, g))-compatible projections, answer more epistemic questions of the type “where are the frame
observables non-trivially realized in spacetime", in which case the answer will typically not be all of M .
Remark 4.17. The fact that we can have Locπ (R) ̸= M is a quantum mechanical counterpart of the idea that in
General Relativity, tetrads need not have a global description and are localised in space and time: only in Minkowski
spacetime do we tend to have globally defined reference frames. That is, we can write (as an analogy)
       There exists a global inertial reference frame ↭ Locπ (R) = M for some “suitable" π.
   It would certainly be interesting to examine the different meanings of different possible π’s, and whether natural
choices arise. We keep this for future work.

    The notion of localizability is however more intricate than in the homogeneous space case. Indeed, if there is an
                                                                                                                  t(α)

in another galaxy if the QRF we have access to is only localised in ours. However, we now show that when both
localisations concur, such a correspondence between relativised and absolute observables does exist.

Remark 4.37. Note that the use of continuous groupoids, as opposed to the more general measurable groupoids, is
relevant precisely here: we make use of ultraweak continuity. The well-definedness of the integrals of relativization does
not rely on such continuity requirements however. It may be that some physical contexts of interest, notably related
to quantum gravity and Wigner’s friend-type scenarios, require the use of non-continuous measurable groupoids, for
which relativization maps are well-defined but do not reduce, even approximately, to any absolute description.
    Note that we associate normal states ρ ∈ D(HS ) of the system to operator fields of states ω ∈ S (HR ) of the
frame; these are two different kinds of states. We have fiber-wise frame preparations giving us probability measures
of frame configurations: these are preparation states of the frame. Normal states are giving us probabilities of
(relativised) observables: these are evaluation states. This is mathematically consistent for the following reason.
Proposition 4.38. Let ρ ∈ D(HS ) and ω ∈ S (HR ). Then the fiber-wise tensor product
                                                Z ⊕
                                       ρ ⊠ ω :=     (ρx ⊗ ωx ) dµ(x)                                                (173)
                                                         M

is a normal state of A(HS ⊠ HR ): ρ ⊠ ω ∈ D(HS ⊠ HR ) ⊂ A(HS ⊠ HR )∗ .
Proof. We first check measurability. Since ρ ∈ D(HS ), the field x 7→ ρx ∈ T (HSx ) is measurable. Since ω ∈
S (HR ), the field x 7→ ωx ∈ D(HR   x
                                       ) is a measurable field of density operators on HR
                                                                                        x
                                                                                          . Hence x 7→ ρx ⊗ ωx ∈

     x      x

trace-class operator in T (HSx ⊗ HRx
                                      ). Moreover, ∥ρx ⊗ ωx ∥1 = ∥ρx ∥1 ∥ωx ∥1 . Since ωx is a density operator,
∥ωx ∥1 = TrHxR [ωx ] = 1. Thus,
                                Z                       Z
                                  ∥ρx ⊗ ωx ∥1 dµ(x) =        ∥ρx ∥1 dµ(x) = ∥ρ∥1 = 1.                       (174)
                                  M                          M

Therefore, ρ ⊠ ω ∈ A(HS ⊠ HR )∗ . It remains to be shown that this predual element is a state. Let B = (Bx )x∈M ∈
A(HS ⊠ HR ) be positive. Then Bx ≥ 0 for µ-almost every x, and hence TrHxS ⊗HxR [(ρx ⊗ ωx )Bx ] ≥ 0 for µ-almost
every x. Therefore                         Z

So ρ ⊠ ω is a positive normal functional. Finally, evaluating on the identity field gives
                         Z

Thus ρ ⊠ ω is a positive normal functional of norm 1 on A(HS ⊠ HR ), i.e. ρ ⊠ ω ∈ D(HS ⊠ HR ).


                                                             31
    Note however that not every product decomposable state in D(HS ⊠ HR ) can be written as ρ ⊠ ω where
ρ ∈ D(HS ) and ω ∈ S (HR ) (e.g. given η ∈ S (HS ) and κ ∈ D(HR ) also gives η ⊠ κ ∈ D(HS ⊠ HR )). Here,
we merely argue that restricting to the consideration of normal states of the joint algebra given by ρ ⊠ ω where
ρ ∈ D(HS ) and ω ∈ S (HR ) is physically meaningful. We argue that computing probabilities of joint observables
in €R (A(HS )) should be done using such normal states.
Remark 4.39. It is here important to distinguish the role of agents and of quantum reference frames. It should not
be assumed that agents are quantum reference frames: agents may (or perhaps may not) always carry a quantum
reference frame. This is a statement that depends on the choice of how to model agents. The operational quantum
reference frames formalism is not a formalism aimed at doing so, though it might help our understanding of the
resources accessible to agents. Rather, one should take a perspectival view here: agents are assumed to be in the
picture and giving a view of both the quantum system S and the quantum reference frame R; it is assumed that
the agent somehow (again, this is dependent on the model of agents) can prepare the quantum reference frame’s
state. The role of quantum reference frames is then to help agents extract information about the system S, very
much in the spirit of measurement schemes [34]. In this sense, the consideration of states ρ ⊠ ω for ρ ∈ D(HS ) and
ω ∈ S (HR ) is natural.

4.4    An example: the canonical groupoid quantum reference frame
Definition 4.2 is a definition, not an existence statement, and it is natural to ask whether generic curved spacetimes,
which motivated the whole construction, actually carry groupoid quantum reference frames. We show in this sub-
section that they do, and that the relevant example is canonical: it is built out of the Haar system alone, requires
no further choices, and has all of the good properties outlined in Definition 4.6. It is the groupoid counterpart of
the ideal frame HR = L2 (G) carried by the left regular representation together with the canonical projection-valued
measure, which plays the analogous role in the group-based theory [7, 19].
    Throughout this subsection (Γ ⇒ M, ν, µ) is a second countable Hausdorff continuous groupoid that can be
equipped with a continuous left Haar system ν = {ν x }x∈M and a σ-finite quasi-invariant Radon measure µ on M ,
as in Sec. 2.6. Recall in particular that supp ν x = Γx for every x ∈ M .
Definition 4.40. The canonical quantum reference frame of (Γ ⇒ M, ν, µ) is the triple

                                       Rcan := U can , ERcan , HRcan                                              (177)
                                                                     

defined as follows.
   1. The field of Hilbert spaces is the field of fibrewise square-integrable functions,
                                 x,can
                                                                         G
                                       := L2 Γx , ν x ,       HRcan :=       L2 Γx , ν x → M,                     (178)
                                                                                       
                               HR
                                                                      x∈M

      whose continuous structure is the one generated by the restrictions f |Γx of functions f ∈ Cc (Γ) [29, 42].
   2. The representation is the left regular representation of Γ: for α : x → y,

                     Uαcan : L2 Γx , ν x −→ L2 Γy , ν y ,        Uαcan f (γ) := f α−1 ◦ γ ,     γ ∈ Γy .          (179)
                                                                                      


   3. The frame observable is the family of canonical projection-valued measures, acting by multiplication by indicator
      functions:
                        ExRcan (∆) := M1∆ ,                                         ∆ ∈ Bor(Γx ).                (180)
                                                           

   The formulas (179) and (180) should be read as saying that the canonical frame is the totality of frame comparisons
available at each point: its fibre Hilbert space is spanned by the arrows of Γx , and its frame observable is the sharp
question “which arrow?”. All of the structure is supplied by the Haar system.
Theorem 4.41. Let (Γ ⇒ M, ν, µ) be as above and ν be source-compatible for µ. Then Rcan of Def. 4.40 is a
principal groupoid quantum reference frame for Γ in the sense of Def. 4.2. Moreover, Rcan is sharp (hence ideal and
localizable) and µ-admissible in the sense of Def. 4.6. If in addition Γ is a Lie groupoid then Rcan is Lie, and if Γ is
bisection-admissible then Rcan is bisection-admissible.


                                                          32
Proof. See App. A.10.
    The canonical frame is, as one would expect of an idealized frame, maximally spread out: it sees every arrow and
localizes nowhere in particular.

Remark 4.43. Prop. 4.42 says that the canonical frame realizes, on an arbitrary curved spacetime, the condition
Locπ (R) = M which was interpreted in Sec. 4.2 as the quantum counterpart of the existence of a global inertial
reference frame. There is no tension with the discussion there: Locs (Rcan ) = M says only “the canonical frame
observable has access to arrows originating at every spacetime point". It is when π is operationally meaningful
(e.g. B-compatible for a subgroup B of bisections) that one interprets such a statement as defining the operational
localization of the QRF in spacetime.
    For the canonical frame the localizing sequences whose existence is postulated in Thm. 4.36 can be written down
explicitly, and crucially they can be chosen to depend measurably on the base point. This is the hypothesis needed
for the global part of that theorem and for the injectivity statement of Thm. 4.30.
Proposition 4.44. Let Γ ⇒ M be a Lie groupoid with continuous Haar system ν, and let Rcan be its canonical
quantum reference frame. Then there exists a sequence ωn = (ωnx )x∈M ∈ S (HR ) of measurable fields of pure fibre

Proof. See App. A.11.
    Combining the above with the results of Sec. 4.3 gives a clean structural statement about the canonical rela-
tivization map.
Corollary 4.45. Let (Γ ⇒ M, µ, ν) be a Lie groupoid where ν is source-compatible for µ, and let Rcan be its
canonical quantum reference frame. Then for every system S = (HS , V ) over M the relativization map
                                                                           Γ
                                         €Rcan : A(HS ) −→ A HS ⊠ HR                                             (184)

is a normal unital ∗-homomorphism. Consequently A(HS ) embeds as a von Neumann subalgebra of the algebra of
Γ-invariant observables of the joint system-frame theory.
Proof. Rcan is µ-admissible and principal by Thm. 4.41, so Thm. 4.30 applies: €Rcan is linear, normal, unital, com-
pletely positive and takes values in A(HS ⊠ HR )Γ . Since Rcan is sharp, item 9 of that theorem gives multiplicativity,
so €Rcan is a normal unital ∗-homomorphism.
Corollary 4.46. Every oriented and time-oriented Lorentzian spacetime (M, g) admits an ideal, general relativistic
quantum reference frame, namely the canonical quantum reference frame of its Poincaré groupoid Poin(M, g) ⇒ M
with respect to the volume measure µg .
Proof. By Prop. 2.11, Poin(M, g) is a transitive Lie groupoid. Prop. 2.28 and Thm. 4.41 then produce a µg -
admissible principal, sharp groupoid QRF for Poin(M, g), which is by definition a general relativistic QRF.


                                                          33
     Corollary 4.46 is the existence statement that makes the constructions of Sec. 6 non-vacuous: relational quantum
fields, relational covariance and the localization limit are available on every Lorentzian spacetime, with no assumption
whatsoever on its isometry group. This should be contrasted with the group-based formalism, which requires a
transitive global symmetry action and therefore has essentially no instances outside the maximally symmetric case.
Example 4.47 (Minkowski spacetime). Let (M, η) be Minkowski spacetime. By Prop. 2.15, Poin(M, η) ∼  ↑
                                                                                                 = P+ ⋉ M,
and the target fibre over x ∈ M is
                                                                    ↑ ∼  ↑
                                   Poin(M, η)x = (g, g −1 · x) g ∈ P+                                 (185)
                                                
                                                                      = P+ .

Under this identification the canonical frame becomes the constant field
   x,can ∼ 2       ↑                                                 ↑                                ↑
  HR      = L P+ ,          U can the left regular representation of P+ , ExRcan the canonical PVM on P+ ,          (186)

that is, exactly the ideal relativistic quantum reference frame of Sec. 5. The canonical groupoid construction therefore
reduces, in the flat case, to the familiar ideal frame of the group-based theory, as it must.
Remark 4.48. The canonical frame is sharp, hence certainly not absolutely continuous with respect to any non-atomic
measure on Γx , in accordance with Thm. 3.18: localizable frames cannot have operator-norm bounded densities. This
is the groupoid manifestation of the tension, discussed in Sec. 3.3, between sharp localizability and regularity of the
frame observable.
Remark 4.49. The canonical frame is an idealization: its fibre Hilbert space L2 (Γx , ν x ) is “as large as the groupoid",
and it should be thought of as a reference object rather than as a model of any physically prepared frame, much as
L2 (G) with the canonical PVM plays that role in the group case.
    The construction uses no dynamical input: the Haar system is a purely kinematical datum. Selecting physically
preferred frames, for instance by restricting to a holonomy subgroupoid determined by the Levi-Civita connection, in
the spirit of Remark 2.10, would produce smaller and more realistic frames.

4.5     Reduction to quantum reference frames for locally compact groups
We now prove that the groupoid formalism reduces to the usual operational theory of QRFs when the Lie groupoid is
an action groupoid. This is a key consistency check of the whole construction. We distinguish two cases: relativization
on a locally compact second countable Hausdorff group G, and relativization on torsors of locally compact second
countable Hausdorff groups. These two cases are often identified as they share many analytic similarities, but the
reduction from the groupoid case is quite different in those two cases.

4.5.1   Groupoid quantum reference frames on locally compact groups
We first start with the locally compact group case. Groupoids reduce to groups when the base space M contains a
single element M = {∗}.
Theorem 4.50. Let G be a locally compact second countable Hausdorff topological group and Γ : G ⇒ {∗} be
endowed with the Dirac measure µ = δ∗ on M = {∗}. Then
   1. R = (U, ER = (ExR )x∈{∗} , HR = {∗} × HR → {∗}) is a δ∗ -admissible groupoid QRF for Γ if and only if
      (UR ≡ U, ER ≡ E∗R , HR ) is a group QRF for G.
   2. S = (V, HS = {∗} × HS → {∗}) is a Γ-system if and only if (US ≡ V, HS ) is a G-system.

   3. D(HR ) = D(HR ) = S (HR ), D(HS ) = D(HS ) = S (HS ).
   4. A(HS ) = B(HS ), A(HS ⊠ HR ) = B(HS ⊗ HR ) and A(HS ⊠ HR )Γ = B(HS ⊗ HR )G .
   5. For all A ∈ B(HS ) and all ω ∈ D(HR ),

                                          €R (A) = ¥R (A),        €R         R
                                                                   ω (A) = ¥ ω (A).                                 (187)



                                                           34
Proof. These statements are immediate from the fact that V ≡ US and U ≡ UR are ultraweakly continuous
unitary representations of Γ = G: fiber-wise ultraweak continuity becomes equivalent to ultraweak continuity when
M = {∗}. Moreover, ER = E∗R is a single POVM which is covariant with respect to Γ = G. No direct integral
is performed, and consequently D(HR ) = D(HR ) = S (HR ), D(HS ) = D(HS ) = S (HS ), A(HS ) = B(HS )
and A(HR ) = B(HR ). µ-admissibility is automatic for µ = δ∗ on M = {∗}. Furthermore, Γ = G so trivially
A(HS ⊠ HR )Γ = B(HS ⊗ HR )G . Finally, the integrals of ¥R (A) (resp. ¥R     ω (A)) are now the same as those of
€R (A) (resp. €Rω (A)), which completes the proof.
    Thus, in this locally compact group case, we recover exactly the standard relativization map: this goes both ways.
We can indeed start from a groupoid QRF and recover a group QRF when the groupoid is a group with M = {∗},
with the groupoid relativization map €R (respectively €R   ω ) reducing to the group relativization map ¥
                                                                                                          R
                                                                                                             (respectively
  R
¥ω ). We can otherwise start from a group QRF and recover a groupoid QRF for Γ = G ⇒ {∗}, with the group
                                                                                             R
relativization map ¥R (respectively ¥R    ω ) becoming the groupoid relativization map €       (respectively €Rω ). As we
now see, the situation is slightly different for the case of torsor QRFs.

4.5.2   Groupoid quantum reference frames on torsors
We now consider the case where Σ =    ∼ G is a topological left G-torsor for a locally compact second-countable Hausdorff
topological group G. Any such G-torsor can be seen as a principal H-bundle q : Σ → M = Σ/H, where H ≤ G is
an admissible14 closed subgroup. Choosing H when several choices are available amounts to a physical choice. This
choice of H is therefore non-canonical, but given a torsor there is always an H for which the reduction is meaningful.
     Choose an origin o ∈ Σ. Every p ∈ Σ has a unique coordinate gp ∈ G such that p = gp · o. Define θo : G → Σ
as the G-equivariant homeomorphism θo (g) = g · o. Define the right H-action on Σ by p · h = gp h · o. This action
is free, and the quotient q : Σ → M := Σ/H is a principal H-bundle. Note that if H = G then M = {∗}, while if
H = {e} then M = Σ.
     The Atiyah groupoid is Γ = Atiyah(Σ → M ) = (Σ× Σ)/H ⇒ M . An arrow [pt , ps ] has source s([pt , ps ]) = q(ps )
and target t([pt , ps ]) = q(pt ). There is a groupoid isomorphism Atiyah(Σ → Σ/H) ∼     = G ⋉ G/H given, after choosing
o, by
                                                [g1 · o, g2 · o] 7→ (g1 g2−1 , g2 H).                              (188)
This is an action groupoid over M = Σ/H. The target fibers are indexed by x ∈ M = Σ/H. For each x ∈ M ,
define

                                             Πox : Γx → Σ                                                                (189)
                                         β = [pt , ps ] 7→ gpt gp−1
                                                                  s
                                                                    · o,      q(pt ) = x.                                (190)

The map Πox is a Borel isomorphism: under Atiyah(Σ → G/H) ∼             = G ⋉ G/H, one has Atiyah(Σ → G/H)x =
{(g, g −1 x) | g ∈ G}. Then Πox (g, g −1 x) = g · o is a homeomorphism with inverse g · o 7→ (g, g −1 x). Even though
the direct integral is over M = Σ/H, each target fiber Γx is identified with the full torsor Σ, not with Σ/H. Thus,
a torsor POVM on Σ can be pulled to a POVM on every Γx .
    Moreover, for an arrow α = [pt , ps ], define κ(α) := gpt gp−1 s
                                                                      ∈ G and set Uα := UR (κ(α)). κ([pt , ps ]) is the
unique g ∈ G such that pt = g · ps . Note that replacing [pt , ps ] by [pt · h, ps · h] for h ∈ H leaves gpt gp−1
                                                                                                                s
                                                                                                                  unchanged.
This relates unitary group representations to the associated action groupoid unitary representation.
    Furthermore, we can embed the system observables as constant operator fields over M : let

                                               ιS : B(HS ) → A(HS )                                                      (191)
                                                    ιS (A)x = A            ∀x ∈ M,                                       (192)

and likewise we can raise the frame observables as constant operator fields:

                                               ιR : B(HR ) → A(HR )                                                      (193)
                                                    ιR (C)x = C            ∀x ∈ M,                                       (194)
   14 A subgroup H of G is called admissible if the quotient map G → G/H is a principal H-bundle. For example, any subgroup of a

discrete group is admissible, and any closed subgroup of a Lie group is admissible [54].

Note that the identity factor is not an additional physical observable: it records that the same group-based operator
is embedded in every base fiber of the groupoid theory. That is, ordinary torsor relativization is the constant operator
field sector of groupoid relativization. Importantly, this is an embedding of the torsor theory into the groupoid theory
for action groupoids, rather than equivalence between the two. This is different to the group case, where both
pictures are equivalent when Γ is a locally compact second countable Hausdorff topological group G.
     We can indeed now have more general groupoid QRFs in this action groupoid case. For example, we can have

                                                                                                              x

                                                                                                                 y

reconstructs the representation UR : G → U(HR ), we can recover a torsor QRF from the action groupoid QRF as
in Thm. 4.51; more generally, we do not.

    Likewise, at the level of the relativization map, we only recover the usual ¥ maps for constant operator fields
Ax = A for µ-almost every x ∈ M , in which case (€R (ιS (A)))x = ¥R (A) independently of x. More generally, even
if the action groupoid QRF R is built from torsor QRF R as in Thm. 4.51 and Vα = US (κ(α)), we have that for

This is now x-dependent. Thus, the groupoidal approach to quantum reference frames extends the torsor formalism
even in the case of action groupoids. Only for certain classes of action groupoid QRFs and for constant operator
fields of the system do we retrieve the standard relativization map given by ¥.


5     Foundations of relational quantum field theory on Minkowski spacetime
In the rest of this paper we apply the groupoid QRF formalism to relational quantum field theory. We proceed in
three stages. First, in this section, we specialize to Minkowski spacetime, where we review the framework introduced
in [22] and link it to Wightman quantum field theory and Algebraic quantum field theory. Next, in Sec. 6, we will
pass to generic curved spacetimes, where the relational field is defined intrinsically on the Poincaré groupoid without
any global trivialization of the frame bundle. Finally, in Sec. 7, we will indicate how the same ideas extend to internal
gauge symmetry via Atiyah groupoids.
    We start by examining the notion of quantum reference frames on the space of oriented inertial reference frames
                              ↑
on Minkowski spacetime O+       (M, η).
                                  ↑                          ↑
Definition 5.1 ([22]). We call a P+ -covariant QRF based on O+ (M, η) a relativistic QRF.
                                 ↑
   A relativistic QRF lives on O+  (M, η), a trivial Lorentz bundle over Minkowski (see Fig. 3). For a frame state
ω ∈ D(HR ), we denote by

                                                         
                                                                              (M, η))
                                                 ↑                                                ↑
the corresponding Born probability measure on O+   (M, η). Note that tetrads are points (x, λ) ∈ O+ (M, η) and are
localised in π(x, λ) ∈ M. We can then likewise discuss the projection of the support of µEωR onto M to discuss the
localization of a QRF R prepared in a state ω.
Definition 5.2. Let R be a relativistic QRF and ω ∈ D(HR ). Let
                                                                                ↑

for the induced probability measure on spacetime. We call

                                                Loc(R, ω) := supp µFωR                                              (226)

the spacetime localization of (R, ω).
    It is easily seen that FR is Poincaré covariant [22]:
                                                                                    ↑

of localization given here is canonical: this is because there exists a global trivialization of the principal bundle, and
                                                                                            ↑

Figure 3: Space of inertial frames for which every point represent a different viewpoint from which physical systems
can be described. A classical inertial reference frame can be thought of as a Dirac delta distribution over this space,
while an oriented relativistic quantum reference frame gives a Born probability distribution over it. The measure

                  ↑
    Let US : P+     → U(HS ) be an ultraweakly continuous unitary representation of the proper orthochronous Poincaré
group describing the kinematics of the system S, and let R be a relativistic QRF. No irreducibility or spin assumption
on US is needed for the construction below. The adjective “scalar” refers here to the absence of an additional finite-
dimensional Lorentz index: the Lorentz orientation is retained as part of the frame variable λ. Wigner’s classification
concerns irreducible positive-energy one-particle representations. If US is such a representation with mass m > 0 and
trivial little-group representation, one may call S a scalar one-particle system of mass m. General field-theoretic and
Fock representations are typically reducible, and all results below apply to them as well.
    A relativistic QRF should not, in general, be assumed irreducible or assigned a sharp mass and spin. A covariant
POVM on the full frame torsor is a system of covariance whose dilation is governed by imprimitivity theory and
is generally not a single Wigner sector. One may impose a spectral-support condition on UR if a definite particle
content of the frame is desired, but this is an additional physical hypothesis and is not used here.
    Fix a bounded operator ϕ ∈ B(HS ). We define its associated Lorentz-oriented absolute field by

                                   ϕ̂· (·) : M × SO↑+ (1, d − 1) → B(HS )                                                     (228)
                                                            ϕ̂λ (x) := US (x, λ) ϕ US (x, λ) .   †

                                            ↑

                                        US (a, Λ)ϕ̂λ (x)US (a, Λ)† = ϕ̂Λλ (Λx + a).                                           (230)

a scalar relational quantum field.


                                                                39
    The absolute quantum field (x, λ) 7→ ϕ̂λ (x) is not directly observable: what is operationally meaningful is its
average with respect to the quantum uncertainty of the frame. In parallel, (scalar) relational quantum fields answer
the question: for ϕ ∈ B(HS ) and R fixed, if given a specific state ω ∈ D(HR ) of the frame, what is the operator
(the observable if self-adjoint) one “sees" using that QRF? This is precisely Φ̂R (ω).
                                                                                            ↑
Remark 5.6. In the above, we omitted the mention of a specific choice of tetrad in O+         (M, η) for the relativistic
QRF and the relativization map (and the relational quantum field). In [22], a physical motivation was given to argue
for this. For now, we will not worry about such a choice, and will omit it for conciseness.
    How does the notion of relational quantum field relate to the “standard" concepts of relational quantum fields

5.1    Relational covariance
The covariance of the frame observable implies a corresponding covariance law for the relational (local) quantum
fields.
                                             ↑
Proposition 5.10 ([22]). For every (a, Λ) ∈ P+ and every ω ∈ D(HR ),

                               US (a, Λ)Φ̂R (ω)US (a, Λ)† = Φ̂R (UR (a, Λ)ωUR (a, Λ)† )                            (235)

and, for µFωR -almost every x ∈ M,

                               US (a, Λ)ϕ̂R             †    R
                                          ω (x)US (a, Λ) = ϕ̂UR (a,Λ)ωUR (a,Λ)† (Λx + a).                          (236)

    Equation (236) is the characteristic relational covariance law: the system transforms actively, but the frame state
transforms at the same time. In general, this is not the naive pointwise scalar covariance law, which is precisely what
allows us to avoid Wizimirski’s no-go theorem [22, 55, 56].


                                                            40
5.2    Localization limit
The formalism reduces to the ordinary non-relational picture when the frame is prepared in increasingly localized
states.
                                                                                                              (x,λ)
Proposition 5.11 ([22]). Let R be a localizable relativistic QRF. Then for any localizing sequence (ωn                )n∈N of
                                      ↑
frame states centred around (x, λ) ∈ O+ (M, η),

                                                lim Φ̂R (ωn(x,λ) ) = ϕ̂λ (x)                                            (237)
                                               n→∞

ultraweakly.
    Thus the absolute Lorentz-oriented quantum field appears as the sharp-frame limit of the relational construction,
in exact analogy with the localization results of Sec. 3.

5.3    Relational causality
The notion of causality can also be expressed in relational quantum field theory, with interesting features arising. No-
tably, the causality properties of relational quantum fields can now depend on the QRF, and one may restrict causality
conditions to operationally meaningful preparations of frames and to “sufficiently" spacelike-separated supports. We
review some of the discussion first presented in [22], and will generalise it in Sec. 6 to curved spacetimes.
    We start by highlighting that the concept of causality is most naturally understood epistemically through the
no-superluminal signalling principle: it is a statement about what can be observed, not what occurs underneath
the carpet. For that reason, we may want to make notions of causality depend on available resources: notably, on
operationally meaningful preparations of the frame (e.g. ω ∈ D(HR ) such that µEωR is compact), and on operationally
meaningful notions of spacelike separations. For example, one can consider notions of spacelike separations for which
an agent can indeed confirm the spacelike-ness of two regions. This is one way to implement smeared light cones;
another is through considerations of quantum gravity. Regardless, this is an interesting notion to work with.
Definition 5.12. Let σ ≥ 0. If U, V ⊂ M, we say that U and V are σ-spacelike separated and write U ⊥σ V if

                                         sup{(x − y)2 | x ∈ U , y ∈ V} < −σ .                                           (238)

Moreover, let R be a relativistic QRF and ω1 , ω2 ∈ D(HR ). We say that ω1 and ω2 are (R, σ)-spacelike separated
and write ω1 ⊥Rσ ω2 if Loc(R, ω1 ) ⊥σ Loc(R, ω2 ).

   In effect, the distinguished family of states SR of the frame R naturally defines a “cut-off scale” for spacelike
separation as follows.
Definition 5.13 ([22]). Let R be a relativistic QRF and SR ⊆ D(HR ) be convex. The quantity

                                 σ(SR ) := inf sup{ (x − y)2 | x ⊥ y ∈ supp µFωR }                                      (239)
                                            ω∈SR

is the SR -spacelike resolution of R.
     This is shown pictorially in Fig. 4. From there, we can discuss relational aspects of causality for relational quantum
fields.

Remark 5.15. Note that a simple generalisation to the following is to consider relational causality for two choices
of QRFs R1 and R2 . This might be meaningful if two agents have access to two different kinds of clocks and rulers.
We however keep such a discussion for future work.
Theorem 5.16 ([22]). Let R be a relativistic QRF, SR ⊆ D(HR ) and σ ≥ 0. If OS ⊆ B(HS ) is

  1. strongly (SR , σ)-microcausal, it is weakly (SR , σ)-microcausal,
  2. weakly (SR , σ)-microcausal, it is (SR , σ)-causal.
    Microcausality is a more ontological implementation than Einstein causality of the no-superluminal signalling
principle. In turn, strong microcausality (causality holding pointwise, regardless of frame preparations) is “more
ontic" than weak microcausality (causality holding pointwise provided frame preparations are spacelike separated).
Arguably, absolute microcausality is the “most ontic" of all those conditions, but is too strong to assume in general
(for σ = 0) by Wightman’s theorem [57]. We see that, in relational quantum field theory, causality is understood
relationally. Some operators may lead to causal relational quantum fields in some QRFs but not others. This is an
interesting specificity of this formalism.
                                                                              ↑
Example 5.17 ([22]). Let R be a relativistic QRF, K be any compact subset of O+ (M, η), Wϕ̂ : S (M, C) → B(HS )
be the Weyl operator for a Klein-Gordon Wightmannian quantum field ϕ̂. Define

                                             R := {ω ∈ D(HR ) | supp µω ⊂ K}.
                                            SK                                                                              (244)
                                                                      ER


Then ∀σ > 0, ∃fσ,K ∈ Cc∞ (M) such that Wϕ̂ (fσ,K ) ∈ B(HS ) is weakly (SK
                                                                        R , σ)-microcausal.




                                                               42
5.4    Relational and Wightman quantum field theory
It may, at first view, be worrying to be able to discuss a notion of “quantum field at x ∈ M" through the relational local
quantum field ϕ̂R  ω (x). Indeed, ultraviolet divergences typically prevent one from doing so. In Wightman quantum
field theory, where quantum fields are (unbounded) operator-valued distributions Φ̂W : S (M, C) → L(D, H), with
S (M, C) the space of Schwartz functions, these     ultraviolet divergences prevent one from assuming these distributions
are regular, i.e. of the form Φ̂W (f ) = M ϕ̂(x)f (x)dd x for a function-independent kernel ϕ̂ : M → L(D, H).

to be function-dependent as follows.

conditions from such analogies.
Remark 5.18. One can see quantum reference frames and relational quantum field theory as providing an operational
meaning to the (arguably obscure) notion of test function in Wightman quantum field theory. These smearing
functions are nothing but the frame smearing functions from the perspective of a quantum reference frame prepared
in a certain state. In turn, given sufficiently “nice" properties of the frame observable ER and/or of the operationally
accessible states SR , these frame smearing functions can be more regular and smooth than just L1 ; perhaps, in
suitable regimes, they are indeed Schwartz, but they need not be in general.
   Overall, the following comparison holds.

Note that, in the Wightmannian framework, the analytic properties of the Schwartz smearing functions (which help
with the analytic continuation of the relevant vacuum expectation values) make σ > 0 causality become equivalent
to σ = 0 causality. This is known as the global nature of local commutativity [58]. The rigidity coming from the
analyticity of these smearing functions is weakened in relational quantum field theory, so σ > 0 causality may not be
equivalent to σ = 0 causality in general. This is an interesting prospect to find new physically meaningful types of
causal quantum fields.


                                                               43
Remark 5.19. At this stage the construction is performed for bounded seed operators ϕ ∈ B(HS ), so the resulting
relational quantum fields and observables are bounded. This is sufficient for the foundation developed here and
already exhibits the basic mechanisms of locality, covariance and localization. The extension to unbounded operators
belongs to a later stage of the programme.
Remark 5.20. Let (fn ) ⊂ S (M, C) be a sequence of Schwartz functions converging (distributionally) to δx . In
Wightman quantum field theory, the limit Φ̂W (fn ) → ϕ̂(x) does not exist (in the space of unbounded operator-
valued distributions): the right-hand side is not an unbounded operator on a Hilbert space [59]. Further note that
S (M, C) ⊂ L1 (M, C)∩L∞ (M, C). That is, from the lens of relational quantum field theory and Sec. 3.3, the fact that
one cannot localize Wightmannian quantum fields is to be understood as the fact that they are described using a QRF
whose frame observable is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure on M and thus not localizable.
Whether it is possible, in relational quantum field theory, to retrieve an analogous version of Wightmannian quantum
fields defined instead over L1 (M, C) and described using a localizable QRF is an interesting open question.

5.5    Relational and Algebraic quantum field theory
One can also generate algebras of relational observables as follows (see [22] for a more thorough exposition and proof
of the following).
Definition 5.21. Let R be a relativistic QRF, SR ⊆ D(HR ) be convex, σ ≥ 0 and OS ⊆ B(HS ) be closed under
adjoints and (SR , σ)-causal. Given a subset U ⊆ M, we call
                               (S ,σ)
                              AOSR      (U) := {Φ̂R (ω) | ϕ ∈ OS , ω ∈ SR s.t. Loc(R, ω) ⊂ U }′′                  (248)

a relational local algebra.
   These relational local algebras satisfy the core axioms of algebraic quantum field theory [60].

Theorem 5.22 ([22]). Let R be a relativistic QRF, SR ⊆ D(HR ) be convex, σ ≥ 0 and OS ⊆ B(HS ) be
(SR , σ)-causal. Then
                                            (S ,σ)          (S ,σ)
   1. (Isotony) For all U ⊆ V ⊆ M, AOSR              (U) ⊆ AOSR      (V).
                                     ↑
   2. (Covariance) For all (a, Λ) ∈ P+ and U ⊆ M,

                                           (S ,σ)                      (U (a,Λ)SR UR (a,Λ)† ,σ)
                              US (a, Λ)AOSR         (U)US (a, Λ)† = AOSR                          ((a, Λ) · U).   (249)
                                     h                  i
                                       (S ,σ)    (S ,σ)
   3. (Causality) For all U ⊥σ V ⊂ M, AOSR (U), AOSR (V) = {0}.

    Thus, just as Wightman quantum field theory can generate algebras of local observables and be seen as a basis
to understand algebraic quantum field theory, so can relational quantum field theory.

Remark 5.23. This can be seen as an algebraic approach to relational quantum field theory – we coin this ARQFT
for short. This shares some similarities with recent work [35, 61] where it was argued that supplementing algebraic
quantum field theory with quantum reference frames can lead to type reduction of the local algebras. These recent
works form the basis of a top-down approach (which we can call relational algebraic quantum field theory, or RAQFT
for short), which contrasts with our bottom-up approach in that in RAQFT, a background spacetime structure is
required to introduce local nets of algebras in the first place. At the moment, (A)RQFT also works on (frame
bundles over) spacetime, but we can plausibly imagine extensions to cases where notions of spacetime disintegrate
but quantum reference frames (and thus relational quantum fields) are still meaningful. This may be relevant in the
context of quantum gravity: one might not be able to talk about a background spacetime structure (i.e. we might
not be able to assign nets of algebras to regions of spacetime), but one can still discuss relations between physical
systems. It would be interesting to understand the links between both approaches, whether they concur on a similar
domain of applicability, and whether RQFT is indeed better suited to discuss quantum notions of spacetimes.




                                                                44
6    Foundations of relational quantum field theory on curved spacetime
We now formulate the curved-spacetime counterpart of the Minkowski construction of Sec. 5. The point is to retain
the operational and relational content of the flat-spacetime formalism while replacing the global Poincaré symmetry
by the Poincaré groupoid of the spacetime. This achieves the passage from a global homogeneous-space description
of frames to a genuinely local, bundle/groupoid-based one.
                                                                                                 ↑
    Let (M, g) be an oriented and time-oriented d-dimensional Lorentzian spacetime. Let O+         (M, g) → M be
                                                                                               ↑
its bundle of oriented orthochronous orthonormal frames, and let Γ := Poin(M, g) = O+ (M, g) ×SO↑ (1,d−1)
                                                                                                            +
  ↑
O+  (M, g) ⇒ M, be the associated Poincaré groupoid. We use the volume measure F        µg of the spacetime (M, g).
The quantum system of interest is described by a continuous Hilbert bundle HS = x∈M HSx → M carrying a
fiber-wise ultraweakly continuous unitary representation V : Poin(M, g) ↷ HS . The groupoid relativization theory
requires no particle-sector assumption on V . Moreover, mass is not classified by irreducible representations of the
transitive Poincare groupoid: such representations are controlled by the Lorentz isotropy representation, whereas the
mass parameter belongs to the orbit structure of the Wigner groupoid (or to the dynamics, for example a Klein–
Gordon operator). Thus a “scalar system of mass m” on a curved spacetime should be introduced only after adding
Wigner-groupoid data or a specific field equation. No such extra hypothesis is needed in the present kinematical
construction, and a general relativistic QRF is not assigned a mass or spin.
Definition 6.1. We call a µg -admissible principal QRF for the groupoid Poin(M, g) a general relativistic QRF.

Lemma 6.2. A general relativistic QRF is bisection-admissible.
Proof. This is immediate from the fact that diffeomorphisms of a manifold preserve the null sets of the volume
measure.

6.1    Relational quantum fields
In the present work, we want to understand how relational quantum fields can emerge from the relativization of
operators (a fortiori observables). A similar picture can be drawn, though we now relativize operator fields ϕ =
(ϕx )x∈M ∈ A(HS ), such that

relativizing operators and recovering a notion of relational observables arising as relational quantum fields. Indeed, it
highlights that the consideration of relational (general) relativistic quantum physics necessarily implies the emergence
of quantum fields, rather than having to postulate them. We now go on to generalise the notion of relational quantum
fields to curved spacetimes. We do this in several steps.

Remark 6.17. In Minkowski spacetime, we see that this recovers the covariance of Prop. 5.10: the groupoid action

Remark 6.22. Note that this notion is now dependent on the choice of π. This is not an issue in the flat spacetime
                                  ↑                                                                    ↑
case, since there is a canonical P+ -compatible projection which is simply the trivial projection π : O+ (M, η) → M of
the trivial Lorentz frame bundle over Minkowski, which determines a canonical Poincaré-covariant spacetime marginal
POVM. There is no such global trivialisation for general curved spacetime, so we now have to specify a choice of
such π.

Remark 6.24. In the group-based setting, where all operators act hon a single Hilbert    i space, the absolute micro-
causality condition with σ = 0 reduces to the familiar requirement ϕ̂λ1 (x1 ), ϕ̂λ2 (x2 ) = 0 for spacelike-separated
points. On a curved spacetime, however, the field operators live in different fiber algebras, so their commutator
is not directly defined. The groupoid microcausality condition transports them to a common fiber before imposing
commutativity. Note however that, in the flat spacetime setting, Wightman’s theorem [56, 62] prevents exact (σ = 0)
absolute pointwise microcausality to hold under mild conditions (the spectrum condition and the existence of a pure
vacuum).
Theorem 6.25. Let OS ⊆ A(HS ) be a set of measurable operator fields closed under taking adjoints, σ ≥ 0, R
be a general relativistic QRF, π = (πx : Poin(M, g)x → M) be a measurable projection, and SR ⊂ S (HR ) be
convex. Then
   1. if OS is absolutely (σ, π)-microcausal, then it is strongly (SR , σ, π)-microcausal (for any such SR ),
   2. if OS is strongly (SR , σ, π)-microcausal, then it is weakly (SR , σ, π)-microcausal,
   3. if OS is strongly semi (SR , σ)-causal, then it is weakly semi (SR , σ, π)-causal (for any such π),
   4. if OS is weakly (SR , σ, π)-microcausal, then it is (SR , σ, π)-causal,
Proof. See App. A.15.
   We can therefore talk about causality in relational quantum field theory in curved spacetime. We keep as future
work the task of constructing explicit nontrivial examples of causal relational quantum fields, as was done in Minkowski
spacetime in [22] using similar notions of causality.
Remark 6.26. It is an interesting open question to understand whether given two B-compatible measurable projec-
tions π1 and π2 for B ≤ Bis(Poin(M, g)), OS ⊂ A(HS ) is (SR , σ, π1 )-causal (resp. weakly microcausal, weakly
semi-causal) if and only if OS is (SR , σ, π2 )-causal (resp. weakly microcausal, weakly semi-causal). That is, whether
the notion of causality is stable under changes of notions of the frame’s localization in spacetime. It would also
be interesting to understand the links between semi-causality and the other notions of causality; in particular, we
conjecture that if R is localizable and there exists localizing sequences centred around the identity in SR , then strong
semi causality implies absolute microcausality, and Einstein causality becomes equivalent to weak microcausality. We
keep such considerations for future work.

   We finally discuss the reduction of the causality conditions. Since Minkowski spacetime itself is a geodesically
convex normal neighbourhood, the curved-spacetime relation x ⊥   ⊥σ y becomes x ⊥σ y ⇔ (x − y)2 < −σ. Let
OS ⊆ B(HS ) be closed under adjoints, SR ⊆ D(HR ) be convex, and define

                                      OS := ιS (OS ),          SR := {ιR (ω) | ω ∈ SR } .                         (299)

In the constant-field sector and with π = π std , the strong and weak microcausality conditions, as well as the Einstein
causality and absolute microcausality conditions, reduce to the corresponding conditions of Sec. 5.
                                                                                        ↑
Proposition 6.27. Let R be a relativistic QRF and R be the general relativistic QRF on O+ (M, η) induced by R.
The following equivalences hold:
   1. OS is absolutely (σ, π std )-microcausal if and only if OS is absolutely σ-microcausal in the sense of Sec. 5;

   2. OS is strongly (SR , σ, π std )-microcausal if and only if OS is strongly (SR , σ)-microcausal;
   3. OS is weakly (SR , σ, π std )-microcausal if and only if OS is weakly (SR , σ)-microcausal;
   4. OS is (SR , σ, π std )-causal if and only if OS is (SR , σ)-causal.
Proof. For β = jz,x (Λ) and γ = jz,y (λ), one has

                                            πzstd (β) = x,        πzstd (γ) = y,                                  (300)
                                        Vβ ϕs(β) Vβ† = US (x, Λ)ϕUS (x, Λ)† = ϕ̂Λ (x),                            (301)
                                        Vγ ψ s(γ) Vγ† = US (y, λ)ψUS (y, λ)† = ψ̂λ (y).                           (302)

               ↑
Since jz,· : O+  (M, η) → Poin(M, η)z is a bijection, the groupoid absolute microcausality condition quantifies over
exactly the same (x, Λ) and (y, λ) as the flat absolute condition. This proves the first equivalence. The equality of
the local kernels, the marginals, and the localizations was proved above, from which the second and third equivalences
straightforwardly follow.
    For the last equivalence, we have that
                 h R         R
                                   i       h     R              R
                                                                        i             h                  i
             JS† Φ̂ (ω1 ), Ψ̂ (ω2 ) JS = JS† Φ̂ (ω1 )JS , JS† Ψ̂ (ω2 )JS = 1L2 (M) ⊗ Φ̂R (ω1 ), Ψ̂R (ω2 ) ,      (303)

and likewise for the adjoint commutator. Together with Eqns. (289) and (290), this proves the equivalence of the
Einstein causality conditions.

    The semi-causality conditions do not reduce to any of the four flat conditions in Prop. 6.27. The flat spacetime

endpoints (rather than just (dd x, dd y)-almost every endpoint), define
                                  n                                                2        o
                                               ↑
                           Pσ := (a, Λ) ∈ P+     | ∃x ∈ M such that (Λ − 1)x + a < −σ .                          (305)

This is precisely the set of Poincaré transformations associated to the arrows whose source and target are σ-spacelike
separated. Indeed, let
                                          Γσ := {α ∈ Poin(M, η) | s(α) ⊥σ t(α)}.                                (306)
                                                                                                              2
Since y = Λx+a, y −x = (Λ−1)x+a. But x ⊥σ y ⇔ (y −x)2 < −σ so α ∈ Γσ if and only if (Λ−1)x+a < −σ.
                                                                                                            

For this stronger pointwise definition, strong semi-causality is equivalent to
                 h                                        i h                                         i
                   Φ̂R (ω1 ), US (a, Λ)Ψ̂R (ω2 )US (a, Λ)† = Φ̂R (ω1 )† , US (a, Λ)Ψ̂R (ω2 )US (a, Λ)† = 0      (307)

for every (a, Λ) ∈ Pσ and every ω1 , ω2 ∈ SR . In either formulations, both versions of relational semi-causality remain
distinct conditions to the other notions of relational causality in the flat constant-field case. In summary, relational
quantum field theory in Minkowski spacetime developed in [22] and Sec. 5 is the constant operator field case of the
more general groupoid-based theory of Sec. 6.

6.5    Towards a locally covariant viewpoint
The construction above is compatible with the general philosophy of algebraic QFT and locally covariant QFT [63–
65]: to a spacetime region U ⊆ M one may associate the algebra generated by operators of the form

                                            Φ̂R (ω),      Locπ (R, ω) ⊆ U.                                         (308)

The groupoid covariance law of Thm. 6.14 indicates that embeddings and local frame changes should act by trans-
porting both the system field and the frame statistics. A fully functorial formulation of the resulting net of relational
algebras will require a separate treatment, but the present section already identifies its basic kinematical building
blocks.
Definition 6.29. Let R be a general relativistic QRF, π = (πx : Poin(M, g)x → M) be BisIsom (Poin(M, g))-
compatible, SR ⊆ S (HR ) be convex, σ ≥ 0 and OS ⊆ A(HS ) be (SR , σ, π)-causal. Given a subset U ⊆ M, we
call
                       (S ,σ,π)
                     AOSR       (U) := {Φ̂R (ω) | ϕ ∈ OS , ω ∈ SR s.t. Locπ (R, ω) ⊂ U}′′            (309)
a relational local algebra for π.
    This construction generalises that of relational local algebras found in Minkowski spacetime (see Def. 5.21 and
[22]) to curved spacetimes.
Proposition 6.30. Let R be a general relativistic QRF and SR ⊆ S (HR ) be convex.                    Then for any b ∈
Bis(Poin(M, g)), b · SR := {ω b | ω ∈ SR } is convex.


                                                           54
Proof. This follows immediately from the fact that Ub is unitary so it preserves convex mixtures.
Proposition 6.31. Let R be a general relativistic QRF, π be BisIsom (Poin(M, g))-compatible, σ ≥ 0, SR ⊆ S (HR )
be convex and OS ⊆ A(HS ) be closed under adjoints. Then for all b ∈ BisIsom (Poin(M, g)), OS is (SR , σ, π)-causal
if and only if it is (b · SR , σ, π)-causal.
Proof. Let ω1 , ω2 ∈ SR be such that Locπ (R, ω1 ) ⊥        ⊥σ Locπ (R, ω2 ). Write ω1b , ω2b ∈ b · SR . Covariance of
BisIsom (Poin(M, g))-compatible marginals gives Locπ (R, ωib ) = φb (Locπ (R, ωi )). But since φb is an isometry, it
preserves the relation ⊥
                       ⊥σ . Hence, Locπ (R, ω1 ) ⊥  ⊥σ Locπ (R, ω2 ) if and only if φb (Locπ (R, ω1 )) ⊥
                                                                                                       ⊥σ φb (Locπ (R, ω2 ))
if and only if Locπ (R, ω1b ) ⊥
                              ⊥σ Locπ (R, ω2b ). Moreover, causality for SR and bisection covariance yields, for any
ϕ, ψ ∈ OS ,                     h                       i    h                    i
                                  Φ̂R (ω1b ), Ψ̂R (ω2b ) = Vb Φ̂R (ω1 ), Ψ̂R (ω2 ) Vb† = 0.                          (310)

The adjoint commutator is identical, which finishes the proof.
